JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOMALIST.

Tettralini.

The appearance at the Hippodrome of a per- former "with Tettrazini voice" set- me won- dering if any of my readers ever saw a Portrait of the eminent Italian songstress on a postcard. • She -came into prominence when the picture poStcard craze was at its height, but, so far as I know, she never condescended to sit for popular circulation. I inquired diligently, making a round of the shops, but, vr late Marie Studholme was to be had in about 150 styles, fiettrazini was not to be bought in that form.

Dutch Experiences. "Miss Hook/of Holland" is being performed at the Royal Court Theatre this week, and I was reminded of another Dutch miss. One of the younger members of my household corre- sponds in Esperanto with a Rotterdam "school marm," and with growing intimacy they have exchanged samples of needlework and so on. But the packages are considerably delayed nowadays, and on delivery they have invariably been "opened by Censor." Whatever "Miss Hook of Holland" may think about it all, -her English correspondent quite recognises that such precautions are essential in this time of war with the super-sneaks of the universe. Mr. E. 0. Greening.

It must be quite half a century ago since I made the acquaintance of this fine old Warring- tonian. • I was in Halifax at the time, and Mr. Greening was nominated as a candidate for the Parliamentary representation of that borough. The old members whom he opposed were Mr. Edward Akroyd, a local commercial-- magnate and philanthropist, and the Right Hon. James Stansfeld, some time president of the Local Government Board, an able statesman who sacrificed his brilliant prospects by his zealous leadership of the opposition to the C.D. Acts, since repealed. Mr. Greening was regarded as an extreme fanatic and firebrand, and his interposition led to much rioting and violence, culminating in his heavy defeat. Much water has passed under the bridges since those days, and Mr. Greening, then an advanced champion of the cause of labour, has been left behind by younger advocates who preach political doc- trines of which even he, enlightened and earnest reformer as he was, apparently did not dream at the period of which I write; at all events, he did not venture to give public expression to such sentiments, the creed which he openly avowed being quite hot enough for the constituency for whose suffrages he vainly appealed. Caveted Russian Order.

I was much interested in the timely hit of information afforded by "Marjorie," her opening paragraph in last week's woman's column, respecting the Order of St. George. I have a young acquaintance who has just been awarded that coveted distinction, and, although he and his comrades are naturally proud of it, the significance of the medal does not appear to be adequately appreciated by our people at home. " Marjorie's " explanation should open the eyes of " Examiner " readers to. the im- portance of the Order; Longevity.

This, I read on page seven of last week's issue. is a hereditary quality, passing from father to son and from mother to daughter. According to my observation, that is by no means in- variably the case. My paternal and maternal grandparents were dctogenarians, so were my wife's, but our parents died in their fifties. Now we are a good way beyond that. I have noticed the like in other family circles. I knew one old couple who had a very large family, but only one of the younger generation attained the fir score years at which age they them- selves passed away. Smart Captures.

Having just read of two of these in last Saturday's issue, I met in the street a super- annuated superintendent of police who was for- merly in one of my 'districts. Ile was still keenly interested in detective work, and we coniptired notes. I may briefly give an example of his cleverpess- when on duty. A stranger had spent a Couple of days at a kcal hotel, and then disappeared with the landlady's jewellery. The police superintendent sound that he had .4:t paramour in a town not far away, and, by having correspondence intercepted, he learned that the thief was to call for a registered packet at a certain post office the morning but one following. The rest was easy, of course. A trusty and experienced member of the forco, was sent to lie in wait for him, and a registered packet containing a halfpenny and a 'co-op" check was addressed to the criminal. It was arranged that when the latter applied for his parcel, the lady clerk at the Pounter should repeat the name, so that the man in hiding might hear distinctly. The thief was required to sign the usual receipt, and having done so, he picked up his letter exclaiming " There ! -That's mine." "Nay, it's mine, and you're mine," said the detective, as he pounced upon his man--who was ultimately induced to disclose the where- abouts of all the missing "swag." Ancient "Ads."

I may supplement last week's example by the following from the " Mercurius Politicus " for the 30th September, 1658:—" That Excellent, and by all Physicians approved, China Drink, called by the Chineans, Tcha, by other nations Tay, alias Tee, is sold at the Sultaness' Head Coffee House, in Sweeting's Rents, by the Royal Exchange, London." That must be fairly familiar, as it has been repeatedly quoted. This example—from the " Tatler " for he 14th September, 1-710—of what Addison terms " the Ciceronian manner" of advertisement wyiting is not so well knowri:—" The highest compounded spirit of lavender, the most glorious, if the expression may be used, enlivening scent' and flavour that can possibly be, which so raptures the Spirits, delights the guest, fine gives such airs to the countenance as are not to be imagined but by those that have tried it."

Concerning Literature. In the paragraph referred to in the fore- going I cited Dr. Johnson, whom Lord Rosebery describes as "‘the John Bull, of English litera- ture," and of whom Adton Smith said "He knew more books than any man alive "—yet he once asked "was there, ever yet anything written by mere man that was wished longer by its readers excepting 'Don Quixote,* 'Robinson Crusoe,' and 'The Pilgrim's Pro- gress'? ". When librarians and other observers have affected to deplore the excessive demand for novels, I have met the objection by suggest- ing that many of the book borrowers possess the-more substantial works which they vatue and merely go to the public library for light reading; and further, I have always maintained that SQ long as a boy acquires the reading habit it is of little moment what he reads for the time being: I am glad now to find my view anticipated and confirmed by this eminent authority. He was asked for advice respecting a boy's reading. "Let him at first read any English book which happens to engage his at- tention," he said, "because you have done a great deal when you have brougtit him to love entertainment from a book. He'll get better books afterwards." When I was a youngster 1 delighted in the penny dreadful and the shilling shocker, and on these I mounted to higher things.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
STAR KINEMA HELPS NAVY DAY

FUND.

As a result of a special performance at the Star Kinema House, Church-street, on Sunday evening, the Navy Day Fund will benefit to the extent of about £lO. There was a crowded audience, amongst which were the Mayor (Alderman Peter Pea- cock), Mr. and Mrs. W. Peter Eylands, and Mr. and Mrs. F. A. Frost. Amongst a fine programme of pictures the "star" one was "The Priest of Isis," a fine production dealing with the last days of Pompeii. Scenes from Warrington Navy Day proved very popular and were heartily ap- plauded. The audience were also delighted with the rendering of several musical items. Madame Armstrong sang, with much effect, "Abide with me" and "The Enchantress," and an orchestra composed of Messrs. S. Apple- ton (bass), IL Whitfield ('cello), 'C. Cook (violin), E. Millington (violin) and L. Osborne (piano) gave several selections with skill. During an interval the Mayor thanked Mrs. Lightburn and the staff of the hall for their services and the public for attending in such large numbers.

WHITE HEATHER DAY.

Wet Weather But Unquenchable

Zeal.

CARNIVAL REALISES £l4O

TO DATE.

At the conclusion of a bright little speech, when she opened the bowling carnival on the grounds of the Arpley Club on Saturday. Mrs. F. Aylmer. Frost promised those present two hours of sunshine in which to empty their pockets. Rain had ceased at the time she made this pleasant announcement, but, unfortunately, the promise of bright skies was not fulfilled, and the remainder of the evening might he described as a season of spasmodic outdoor amusement. Showers were frequent and im- pressive, but they could not damp the ardour ' of the workers or of those who came to support the cause. Bowling matches would be in full swing, when suddenly a rattling downpour would make the players resume their jackets and stampede for shelter. At these unpleasant intervals the pavilion, refreshment tent and other havens of refuge were crowded to over-. flowing with hundreds of rather disappointed but. always good humoured rain dodgers. - Prompt to the hour of opening the military band of the Royal Ancient Order of Buffaloes marched on the ground, playing a lively tune. A goodly company gathered round the club- house for 'the opening ceremony. His Worship the Mayor asked -Mrs. Frost to open the proceedings, and said they were 01l grateful that she had found time to be with them that afternoon. Mrs. Frost humorously remarked that an apology was due to her for asking her to open the carnival, as she was not Scotch, although she wore a bit of white heather. But she felt it a great honour. Their Scotch friends were a magnificent race—a fighting race and no slackers. They admired the race and also their beautiful country. (Hear, hear.) She went .in to explain the object of the carnival—to raise funds, first 'of all, for the premier institution of the town, their Infirmary, which should be specially remembered in their anxiety at the present time to do what they could for their wounded and sick soldiers, she had the honour to be one of the ladies' committee. , Then they had the District Nursing Association, through which nurses were sent out to the homes of the people of Warrington. That equally,, needed their support. Lastly, they had the Children's Summer Camp. These children i would be the Mothers and fathers of the future, and would need all they could do for them. She would ask them to do what they could that afternoon to make the carnival a success. (Applause.) Mr. W. Murray, president of the Caledonian Association, proposed a vote of thanks "to the worthy and distinguished lady, Mrs. Frost, who had come to open the proceedings." She was, he said, industrious and energetic in every movement for the welfare of the' people of War- rington at large. She was striving to aid every movement that had as its object the benefit of humanity. Long might she be spared in her efforts on behalf of these charitable institutions. Mr. J. Willett (chairman of the Works' Bowling Association) seconded the vote of thanks, and said Mrs. Ftost was always in the forefront of any movement for, the interests of the people in the town. The vote was carried most cordially. • Mr. J. L. Davenport (vice-president) mov-..d a'vote of thanks to His Worship the Mayor for presiding, and this sentiment was also received in a flattering manner.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD DAY'S WORK.

It was found that although hundreds of people must have been deterred by the weather from coming to tile carnival, £l4O was realised during the day. There have still to come :n the proceeds of draws for a gold watch and a gas cooker. Everyone wore a sprig of white heather. The bonnie flower,had-come all the way from Perth. There were large bundles of the bloom, and the sprigs were made up in the Infirmary by several ladies. The distribution was ably taken charge of by Messrs. J. IL Smethurst and H. Collins, assisted, amongst others, by the following ladies: Mrs. ^W. Patrick, Mrs. J-Tennant, Mrs. R. Straw, Mrs. H. Hope, Mrs. McDermid, Mrs. W. Mitchell, Miss Ashilown and friend, Miss Pownall, Miss Laiety, Mrs. Burdett, Mrs. Eaton, Mrs. D. Reid and Mrs. W. Stirling. The stalls and amusements were energetically managed by those in charge. Northern Anglers' Association Stall.—Mr. Potter. _

Houp-la.—Miss H. Cox. ,Circular Houp-la.—Mr. J. Smith and the Xis ses Collin. Cocoanut Shies.—Mr. Hackney and Mr. Wick- ham. Swings.—Mr. A. Meyer and Master Allen. Aunt Sally.—Messrs. Stout, R. Straw, J. Burdett and J. Robertson. Donkeys.—Master D. Tennant. Sweets and Lemonade.—Mr. and Mrs: A. J. Allen and party. Draws for Gold Watch and Cooker.—Mr. and Mrs. George Shaw. The half-hourly concerts and variety enter- tainments were a great success and the Brook- lyn Glee Party are to be highly congratulated on their efforts. When the evening was most uninviting the seats were taken inside the pavilion: and quite a large number were accom- modated. Gus Garrick and Leslie Carter came over from the Hippodrome and got a great re- ception. The following contributed to the pro- grammes :—Madame Sara Silcock, Madame Eckersley, Mr. J. Sephton, Mr. A. Pearce Mr. T. Crowe, Mr. W. Dutton and Mr. G. Macklin (humorist). Mrs. Fairhurst was at the piano, The concerts were arranged by Messrs. Lambert, W. Murray. .G. Walker, J. Bennett, E. Wallington and W. H. J. Jenkins, the first named being particularly idefatigable in his efforts in this direction as well as for dancing. A much-enjoyed treat was the Highland dances, in costume, by the Misses Stoddart, Mr. James Scott playing the pipes. The Buffaloes' Band, under the leadership of Mr. Robert Smith, played nice selections of- dance music, Messrs. W. Murray, Cook and 'Lambert acting' as M.C.'s. The cash stewards were Mr. H. Hope, Master R. Hope, Mr., Pemberton and Mr. W. Daintith, and the hand, steward Mr. J. McDer- mid. Messrs. R: Smith, J. Burdett, A. Meyer and G. Walker acted as stewards, and the work of Mr. J. Tennant and Mr. R. Cook, hon. joint secretaries, deserves all praise. '

THE BOWLING.

The grand bowling match between the Works' Bowling Association and the Caledonian. Association (assisted) resulted in a victory for the former team. 'Owing to the weather it was impossible to proceed with the Three-a-side Competition. The first round only was Com- pleted and it was decided to postpone the match until September 23rd, when it is hoped the competition will be / concluded on the same green. The draw for the second round will appear next Saturday. The• Ladies' Competition was arranged by Mrs. R. Straw, Mrs. Eaton, Mrs. D. Reid; Mr. R. Straw, Mr. P. H.- Brown and Mr. J. Bennett, and the prize-winners were :-1, Mrs. Brooks; 2, Mrs. Ford; 3, Miss Cooper; 4, Miss Messiter; 5, Mrs. Evans; 6, Mrs. Morrison; 7, Mrs. Anson; 8, Miss Eaton. Mrs. Murray presented the prizes. Mr. Patrick proposed a vote of thwaks to Mrs. Murray and to those who gave. the prizes, the motion being seconded by Mr. McDermid, and carried with acclamation.

Mrs. Murray responded. The following conducted and arranged the bowling matches :—Messrs. Patrick, R. Smith, Bowman, Clayton, Brown, Robertson, Howarth and Marshall.

The donors of prizes for ladies' main were: Councillor W. IL J. Jenkins, Messrs. Eustance and Co., Mr. T. Finlayson, Mr. Hallett, Mr. H. Collin, Mr. H. Hope, Mr. Milner, Mr. G. Walker, Mr. A. J. Allen.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 2, 1916.

THE VILLAGES IN WAR TIME.

Countryside Mothers' Great

Sacrifice For Nation.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
UNNAMED HEROINES OF

ENGLAND.

By GEORGE A. LEACH.

If you want fully to realise that the British nation is at war, spend a few days in some quiet little village. Stand at midnight, under the old village oak, and listen. You will hear the prayers of those big-hearted mothers; the unnamed heroines of England. I can imagine these simple souls standing in the centre of a city in war-time. I can see" the look of stern reproof as they watch the frivolous laughing crowds leaving the kinema show and the music- halls. • •

You! You big-hearted country mother, you need no evening paper to remind you of the war. There is more of the reality of the world's greatest tragedy in that crude, pencil- written letter from your son or sons, than in all the official dispatches of the Press. You have had time; in your quiet country life, to rear your lads, and now you have .time to think; There is no artificial counter-attraction

for you. Even Nature, in all her beauty; is cruel. The heavier crop of weeds your cottage garden yields reminds you of those brown hands now ceaselessly clutching a rifle or throwing a grenade. The rattle of the reaper, as it ploughs its way through the golden fields of corn, re- minds you again. How often do you listen for his strong, hearty shout as the rabbits bolt -

from the dwindling Cornfield square. Mary envies the thrush of his love-song, for she no longer meets Jack by the old stile; the world does not share her tears as she walks the old field paths alone.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
BROOK STILL SINGS, BUT THE BOYS ARE GONE.

Feeling the need of a change, in these strenuous times, I paid a visit, a short time ago, to the village of my birth. Unmolested, I allowed my mind to wander as I walked those dear old Cheshire lanvs of one's schooldays. I thought of old school chums as I passed the little plantation where we used to pay a game of "Fox and Hounds!' The brook where we found "lucky-stones" still sings a merry song. The old beech trees still bear the scars of our pocket knives;' and 'some of the initials upon their trunks are still decipherable. The old "Daresbury Firs, where broody pheasants were oft disturbed, renriins to remind one of Gamekeeper Jones' "drubbing's" for schoolboy delinquencies. I was filled with happy memories as I reflected on the glorious days of the past. Lightning-like, however, past memories gave place to the real issues of to-day. These lads of the corduroy and red scarf had donned the colours of their King and country. Often they had "played soldiers";' now they had joined their 'comrades to' fight in the battle of free- dom. Often, in the village school, they heard of France, Belgium, Egypt, Mesopotamia and Greece. But little did they think that one day they would tread foreign soil. And they have gone with brave hearts, too. Go into the homes of these country lads. Talk with their parents, and you will fully realise the stuff of which they are made. In one cottage there resides an old' man of eighty. years; "Grannie,'' the lads called him. He holds a much-coveted memento of the great wear—a German officer's helmet. With big tears streaming down his weather-beaten face he will hand it to you for inspection, but certainly not for "keeps." A derman helmet in a little Cheshire village! Spoils from the greatest war in the history of the world. The bended knee of a Prussian bully before the bayonet of a "Country Johnnie." Bravo! the lads of the countryside. Walk a little farther down the road and a father will relate with pardonable pride and sorrow how 'his lad went down in the North Sea fliodlt.

THE LOSSES AND THE HOMECOMING. Go and listen to the mother whose boy died from malaria in the marshes of Mesopotamia. "She is .sorry ‘, she Jost.- her boy, but she'd rather he had died fighting." , - Go and see the mother whose three boys have gone, giving her sympathy to the mother of an only • son. Yes! These big-hearted, modest mothers fully realise the gravity of the situa- tion. And more honour is due to them than they receive. "If it were passible to break up this medal into a hundred pieces and distribute it amongst my comrades, that is how it ought to be." These words are from Rifleman Proctor, V.C. (Liverpool Regiment), spoken at the civic re- teption •accorded him a short time , ago in Liverpool. No one will grudge this brave soldier the full value of the great honour con- ferred upon him. But, what is true of Rifle- man Proctor's reference to his comrades, is also true of the mothers of England. Full-earned honour is the child of sacrifice. And who, above all others, have rendered greater sacri- fice than the mothers of our gallant soldier boys? _

The villages of the British Isles are render- ing their country noble service. The big- hearted mothers of the countryside have in reality given their all. Over long years of pain and poverty they have borne a heavy load. Many times, hungry themselves, they have fed the bairns. Unselfishly they have fitted them' to battle for themselves. And now, in their declining years, they have made a supreme sacrifice, a sacrifice of their own flesh and blood on the altar of National Duty. The mother has given with scarce a murmur. She has shared her tears with those who have already lost their lads. She reserves the rest as tears of joy for those who return. Patiently, she is longing for the homecoming. There will be no banners flying; no processional bands to lead the boys of the countryside home again. No! None of these things are awaiting you, my old schoolmates. The old men will doff their hats and 'claim you as British heroes. The open arms of your great - mothers are waiking 'to ileceive you. They will press you to hearts biting with joy. And as they lay down to restrthey will say----"`We gave our all."

A PARTING SHOT.

Pacifist Penketh Minister

Retaliates.

SHOULD THE CLERGY

ENLIST?

The Rev. H. T. Dixon, the Penketh Wesleyan minister, whose invitation to stay another year was cancelled because of his attitude to the war, had much to say of his critics on Sunday even- ing, when he delivered his farewell sermon. His subject, "The Churches and the War," had been made widely known, and the church of which he had been the pastor for two years was crowded. Anti-militarists from all over the Warrington Circuit sat in the pews and, along with sympathetic members of the Inde- pendent Labour Party and the No-Conscription Fellowship, uttered many "Hear, hears"

throughout the sermon. At the beginning of his discourse the reverend gentleman wanted the congregation plainly to understand that with the war politically he had nothing to do as he stood in the pulpit, as it was an understanding in Wesleyan Methodism that all polities should be kept out of the pul- pit. He went on to remind them that just be- fore the war orgonised Christianity—in the pulpit, on the platform, and in the Press— greatly condemned a celebrated playwright for introducing into one of his plays the expression "Bloody." About the same time there was an increased interest shown in prize fighting, and thousands of sermons were preached and leading articles written in all the religious newspapers condemning that sport in the strongest terms---declaring that it was stocking in its brutality and demoralising even to wit- ness such a spectacle., Just at that time the European War broke out. "Now it is a strange psychological fact that

_ the leaders of the Christian Churches at once joined the war party and shouted as loudly as any for a fight to a finish, even though it meant the last man and the last drop of blood," de- clared Mr. Dixon. "Those who condemned the use of the red adjective and were shocked be- yond measure at the brutality of prize-fighting, supported enthusiastically: the war and cheered all the combattnts. . . . I think if ChriA were on earth he would have something to say about straining at a gnat and swallowing a camel." Some within the . Churches had remained faithful—there were the 7,000 in Israel who had not bowed the knee to Baal—and he believed that the majority of the members of the Churches were sound at heart, though the Churches as a whole were stampeded by a few leading officials. The Churches officially, i.e., as represented by the clergy and ministers, the leading officials, and religious Press, had be- come drunk with the war spirit. Churches had been turned into recruiting depots, Jingoism had taken the place of the Gospel, the God of Love had been dethroned and Mars set up in His place, and the leaders of the Churches, like ' Saul of Tarsus, had been breathing out threatenings and slaughter. "A Shameless Proceeding."

Had the message of the brotherhood of man and the /Fatherhood of God been preached, generally speaking, during the past two years? No. Instead, there had been reintroduced the old conception of a Tribal Deity and the doc- trine of the universal brotherhood of man had _ _

been frankly abandoned by some who declared that it was not only an impossible idea, but was also undesirable. If those who had preached such a message had frankly abandoned Christianity and adopted Pagan principles that would, at least, have been honest, but to take the beautiful message of Christ and so distort it as to make it mean the Very opposite of what it said, was a shameless proceeding. At the beginning of the wnr a celebrated writer sug- gested that the Churches should be closed until the .war was over, just as the Temple of Peace in Rome remained closed during *ar, and if the Churches had not the courage to deliver 'their proper message during war, he (the preacher) thought also it would be far better for them to be shut- up until kostilities had concluded. (Hear, hear.).: .

If it ,-had not been for the Churches this war would have been like any other—just a/struggle for domination. But the Churches had given their benediction to it and given it a religious incentive. They had declared that it was not a war in the ordinary sense, but a great spiritual struggle between the Powers of Light and of Darkness, that Christ Himself had put the sword into our hands, and that if He had been on earth He would have enlisted. "Well now," commented the reverend gentle- man, "if this is a great spiritual struggle and if Christ Himself would enlist, then the place of every minister and clergyman is in the trenches. (Hear, hear.) But you are all familiar with what, has taken place. When an . . .

invitation was sent, out to all ministers and clergymen of military age to join the . Army, some responded, but they were only a compara- tive few; in fact, I was reminded of what our Lord said in one of his parables, 'They all with one consent began to make excuse.' Instead of saying, like Isaiah, Here am I, send me!' they said, 'Send somebody else.' At length the bishops came to the rescue and forbade the clergy to enlist: so when the Conscription Bill came out the Anglican clergy found theniselves exempted, and, of conrse,,Nonconformist minis- ters had to be/ put on an equality with their Anglican brethren and they, too, were exempted. I have not heard any of my ministerial brethren complain of being left out." (Hear, hear.) The reverend gentleman went on to contrast the timidity with'which the Churches protested against. social wrongs and the enthusiasm dis- played over the war and, in conclusion, he Oe- dared that they had a great worc to do' when the war was over. If they would only have the courage to do their nroper work and proclaim their true message they could look forward to the future with confiderice.

PRESENTATION TO MR. DIXON.

Offers To Conic Back To NVatTington

In An Unpopular Cause.

, On Saturday evening a social gathering ,of friends of the Rev. H. T. Dixon took place at Atkinson's Cafe, at which he was made the recipient of a gift prior to his leaving this circuit.

PATRIOTISM NOT A CHRISTIAN VIRTUE. Mr. A. Mountield presided, and in the course of an address said that the gathering repre- sented many Churches and friendships. It was a meeting composed of those who desired to show Personal homage and respect to Mr. Dixon. They admired him as one who had endeavoured to See Christian teaching steadily and see it whole. Pew, teacters in these days, or in any age, had been able to do that. Indeed there were fundamental Christian doctrines which had never yet been grasped or taught by the Church. One of these was Paul's reiterated taching that there is neither nationality nor sex nor caste in the Christian scheme. As tte Apostle taught it, there was neither .Tew nor Greek, because all were the children of God. The Church, in. contradiction to this, had put its 'blessing upon nationality, and in this had made one of its greatest blunders. Patriotism, though preached from thousands of pulpits, was not a 'Christian doctrine nor a Christian virtu".

They could only hope that the coming days, with the vigorous 'thinking that would follow the war, would bring this home to them. Many things were already in process of 'discovery. They were discovering the terrible logic of war. Once the nations resorted to force there was no logical stopping place short of utter. extermina- tion.' The Germans were quite logical in their doctrine of "frightfulness "; we were equally so in adopting conscription, and the conscrip- tion of wealth and youth and age and woman- hood must all follow unless we give up the war, for it is all in the process. They were also coming to see that the choice was between two alternatives, and that there was no middle course. They could adopt war which offered savagery as its method and a continent of wreckage as its result, or on the other hand they could accept the example and teaching of Jesus, who denounced the use of the sword and bade them trust entirely in righteousness and love. The presentation, which took the form of a

Continued in next column.

A PARTING SHOT.—Continued.

wallet of notes, was then made by Mr. Moan field.

MR. DIXON "FLABBERGASTED."

Mr. Dixon; acknowledging the gift, said it was altogether unexpected; he had no idea until a short time ago that the gathering was being got together. " Flabbergasted " expressed fully how lie felt by the presentation. He thought he would feel more comfortable if surrounded by a host of foes; such kindness and good will took all the wind out of one.

It is very difficult to express what one would like to say at such a time, but he wanted them to believe that he very much appreciated the gathering, the kind words that had been expressed, and their handsome present. He would carry away very many pleasant memories of Warrington; he was proud of those with whom he had been associated. He was proud, too, of the causes that are at present unpopular with which he had been associated. He was altogether unworthy of what had been said and what had been done, especially when they thought of those who had not been in the limelight so much, but who, in an obscure way, had suffered for their convictions as he had not been called upon to do. Moreover, they had had to submit to trying' ordeals such as he had not been called upon to face. These were the people worthy of their honour. Indeed, they were the type of men of which any civilisation might proud. "I look forward,'' continued Mr. Dixon, ." to the time when money, gold or notes shall not be needed at all, when, this beautiful world shall be the common property bf all its in- habitants. I look forward to the time when the motto adopted by Thomas Paine, 'The world is my country, mankind my brethren, to do good my religion,' shall be our motto. I value this present because it comes from such a representative gathering, not merely from one denomination or one section of the Church, but from many Churches and denominations and different parties. I value -it especially because of the good will and friendliness which it represents. I thank you very much, and in the coming days if I can render any Service to any good cause in Warrington, especially if it hap- pens to be an unpopular one, I shall be only too pleased to come." Mrs. C. Cooke, Messrs. H. Gray, H. Wardman, A. Seabury, W. E. Lowe and S. Gough spoke, testifying to the esteem in which Mr. Dixon is held in Warrington. - -

The miwical programme, which included songs by Miss E. Tanner, Miss M. Whitmore and Mr. A. Pearce was very much appreciated.

A KHAKI RIBBON.

South Lancashires' Departure From Bank Quay.

MEMORABLE SCENES.

Although the emotion of leave-taking must have been keenly felt after the happy furlough they had spent in their abode of pre-war days, a battalion of the South Lancs. • Regiment left Warrington on Sunday afternoon, full of buoy-. ancy, hope, and enthusiasm which should always characterise soldiers of the King. The scenes of departure will remain memor- able. Long befOre a prime-lunged bugler sounded the " Fall-in " at 4.30 the avenues leading to the Drill Hall held more people than I they could comfortably accommodate, and the men in khaki had to force their way to the scene of assembly. ' One of the first on parade after his furlough was Jack, the regimental mascot—an Airedale Terrier who ran about with the authority of a/ General and the speed of a dispatch rider. As the men lined up in companies, however, his friskima. was curbed and. "No. 1" of the transport section took' him in his arms and blessed him. The least indication of restlessness either of body or vocal power and a swarthy hand muzzled him into silence. Jack obeyed readily: he had probably heard stories of what happens in the guard- room. _

Assembled, the battalion marched to the lawn in front of the Town Hall, where they were arranged in 'quarter-column order with the bugle and drum band ori thN Alght. The N.C.O.'s had more checking of numbers to do, and Major McClure took advantage of the interval to " snap " the khaki mass from the Town Hall steps and, later, also to photograph some of the men who have served in the local Territorials and been discharged. The jolliest of these was one with a leg off. He looked proud of his badge and ribbon. Prominent citizens had thronged the Town Hall steps and Major Chorley, who was in comL mend of the battalion, signalled to tne Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) that all was ready for him to .address them. Having congratu- lated them upon their smart appearance he urged them to do their duty fearlessly, fear God and honour the King. He wished them all the good luck that Warrington could give them and hoped that it would not be long before they were back home again. Hearty cheers were .then raised, the volume being ' swelled by people as far distant -as Sankey-street.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KHAKI RIBBON UNROLLS.

The move-off—an order given and carried out with precision—was the signal for a great demonstration of cheering. As the long khaki ribbon rolled down Sankey-street and Parker- street to Bank Quay Station the civilian throng cheered and cheered again, waving their hats and umbrellas (for it had been raining) in passionate enthusiasm. When people found admission to the station was barred, thousands rushed into Arpley fields to see the train start for the South, but thousands upon thousands remained and, as the men in khaki approached, the police were , powerless to maintain the space requisite for ,an orderly entrance into the station. Only barricades could have done that. With an irresistible rush the Great B.P. flooded tha square and protesting Specials declaimed futilely. There was a clash between civilians and soldiers—good-humoured and almost apologetic, of course: certainly affectionate—and all mingled together in hopeless confusion. The " Tommies " had to fight their way into the station. The big drum had to be held aloft— and so had Jack, who barked vigorously. On the station the men indulged in frequent cheering and singing—the latter to the accom- paniment of a cornet. The emotion of " Fare- well, remember • me" were soon counteracted by rag-time. Just before leaving "Auld Lang Syne " was sung, and. as. the train slipped away patriotism was symbolised in the singing Of "God Bless the Prince, of Wales." And God bless them, too.

150 WOUNDED SOLDIERS AT ARPLEY.

The members of the Warrington Cricket, Bowling and Tennis Club 'invited one hundred and forty • wounded soldiers from' the various hospitals in the town and district to their grounds in Arpley on Monday for tea and recreation. The weather, which had been un- p'ropitious during the, previous fortnight, was very favourable for he occasion, and, a most enjoyable time was spent in games of competi- tion, including football, cricket, bowls, tennis, 9lock-golf, quoits, &c. The lads entered heartily into all the sports, and, at the close expressed the great pleasure they had derived from their visit.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

THE THREE-SPEED GEAR. No invention of recent years: except perhaps the introduction of pneumatic tyres, has proved so beneficial to cyclists as that of the three-speed gear. It increases thd pleasure of cycling to a degree which cannot he realised by those who have not this excellent contrivance fitted to their machines. It is no exaggeration to say that the three-speed gear has added a. new zest to cycling, and has done much to make the pastime so enormously popular. Long stretches of level road are no longer monotonous, while hills have lost their terrors. The weight of the hub with three gears and kop tube control is well under 31b. An ordinary free- wheel and hub weigh nearly 111 b., so that the extra weight added to the cycle is only a pound and a half. The three gears are worked upon what is called the "sun and planet" system, a series of cog and gear wheels working into' each other inside the rear hub of the cycle, or in a gear box in the case of a tricycle. In ascending a .hill the low gear should be used, enabling the rider to mount with ease. If the hill be a long one he would commence with drop from top to middle speed until the strain becomes great, when he would slip into low gear, and so without stopping for a moment would easily lurmOunt the hill. The 'exact gears may vary, of course, in accordance with the requirements of the rider. The ones I find most convenient are 54- inch, 71-inch and 94-inch. I think this combination is the most valuable.

WHEN A SCREW BREAKS. One of the most distracting jobs I know of in connection with a car is the removal of a screw, the head 45f which has broken' off Short: Owing to one of the back doors of my car being accidentally left open as I was, coming out of the garage a few days ago the hinges were wrenched off and the screws snapped, off close to the head. Some of the screws were broken off so short that it was impos- sible to cut a slot for the screw-driver with a hack saw, the method that should always be adopted when feasible. The way, I removed tha remaining screws was to drill a hole in the cen.re of each, in- serting-the point of a small triangular file. A little pressure and a turning movement removed all save one. So firmly was this in position the file refused to remove it. In this case the only way to overcome the difficulty was to drill a hole in the centre with a left-hand screw, and screw in a screw to match. The act of screwing up the latter had the effect of unscrewing the broken piece. r BOOTS VERSUS SHOES.

Quite a large number of fellow cyclists one meets on the road nowadays are wearing boots, in spite of the fact that it is one of the unwritten laws of cycling that shoes should always be worn in pre- ference to boots. Not only are they more com- fortable, but they enable the rider to use his ankles to much better advantage. When boots are worn for cycling it is next to impossible to secure full use of the ankles. OLD-AGE PENSIONS.

Sir,—l have been reading lately of the hard- hips of old-age pensioners. It is a disgrace to a Christian country like England to give a Paltry ss. a week to poor old respectable people. Their circumstances ought to be looked into and something more done for them. Mr. Marold Smith could put this question before the members of the House of Commons. We -Want a few more men like Mr. Will Crooks, M.P. for Woolwich. We ought to make the Old people's lot as comfortable and happy as We possibly can. The age limit ought to be 65 instead of 70.

NICHOLAS NICKLEBY

===== 0002601_19160902_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
ECONOMICS AFTER THE WAR.

Sir,—ln 'commencing this letter I quote again from Sir Algernon Firth, who says :" The ar has opened our eyes to many things. It "as shown us the weakness of our Empire as well as its potential strength. We hav€e had no co-ordinated policy and strategy; no prepar- ation for war; no preparation in financial %afters; no regulation in shipping services; ollrganisation of our food supplies. We have IlaT no constructive Imperial policy with re- gard to trade and commerce, nor any organised attempt to develop our trade and protect in- dustries of vital importance to the country. When, the war is over our Empire must discard the old system of drift. It must ha% e a defi- nite policy which will secure to the British race the fruits of its past sacrifices and efforts and the due reward for the heroic deeds of all its sons."

Taking my theme from the above, quotation and reverting to the question of raw materials, Which are the bed-rock foundation of national Prosperity, let me point out how, to a certain extent, matters might be remedied. It is to the advantage of a country to put as much labour as possible into its raw material, with view of increasing the intrinsic value of its exports. In inverse ratio it should be its Object to encourage partly manufactured goods ija Preference to the .completed article to keep down the cost of imports. In other words, traPorts and exports should balance each Other. Do we get the most we ought from our raw material? Most certainly not.

-To-take only a few samples of raw materials we get nothing from them beyond their export value as raw material, while we purchase back the finished article from the foreigner at a considerably increased price. There is the Wolfram ore and monazite shipped from India in its raw state to Germany, and re-purchased LY us in its finished state from Germany. There is nickel in Canada, 87 per cent. of Which goes to the United States to be refined from whom we have to purchase our supplies. There are palm kernels from West Africa, the raw material of the margarine trade, almost wildly taken to Germany and Rolland, and re- exported to England in its finished state. Even in the case of pharmacy and dye stuffs the raw material is British coal. On the other hand, take the case of sugar. Our annual imports of Sugar amounts to .1,23,000,000, of which only £930,000 worth comes from British possessions; -while in the year before the war Germany and Austria alone supplied A:15,000,000 worth. The Whole of the sugar required could be produced Within the Empire, cane sugar in the tropics, and beet sugar in the temperate zones; the angar beet can be profitably grown in England. Another raw material, the increased growth of Which might be encouraged within the tmpire, is cotton. The chief cotton-producing eonntries are the United States, China, India, and Egypt; and, it must be borne in mind, that in pre-war times the demand for cotton exceeded the supply. In 1894 and 1898 the Price of cotton .at Liverpool was below 3d. per lb., while in the autumn of 1913 it was worth 7-26 d. per lb.; a crop of this character will be worth cultivating in suitable areas within the tinpire. There, are numerous other nuts and kernels and vegetable fibres produced within 'the Empire, and which might be worked up Within the Empire, but it is unnecessary to discuss them as sufficient has already been said t 0 show the drift of matters.

Row are we to retain these raw materials Within the Empire? By 'lmperial preference Rnd by the inter-trading with each /other of ,the various sections of the British Empire. We nave three good examples in the recent steps taken in West Africa with ,palm nut kernels, and in Australia with sugar and metals. In the case of palm kernels the -West African Government imposed an export duty of per ton, to be remitted on all kernels 'crushed Within the Empire; this has had the effect of encouraging the erection of crushing mills Within the Empire, having the double-effect of creating a demand for suitable machinery, and bY the residuum from crushing providing etock-breeders with cheap feeding-stuff. In Australia, in regard to sugar, the Common- wealth Government has entered into arrange- ents with the growers, k millers and refiners in cbe sugar beet industry for a uniform profit to each ,for a number of years, guaranteeing that ..the general public during the same period shall be able to purchase beet sugar at 31d. per lb. In regard to Australian metal, it appears that Mr. Hughes has induced the Imperial Government to purchase from Australia during he war and for ten years afterwards 100,000 .).`clbs of zinc concentrates annually. The 13113erial Government further lends to the vommonwealth, on War Loan terms, 0t500,000- for the erection of the necessary plant to pro- 'Nee spelter, and undertakes to, make an annual purchase of 4-5,600 tons of spelter. _The following interesting item on how the Jiernians obtained the monopoly in monalite .ns disclosed by Mr. S. J. Johnstone in a l'EtPer at the annual meeting of the Society of Chemical Industry in Edinburgh. Church Reims,

TO BE A VICAR.

Rev. J. M. Buckmaster Going To Wigan In November.

CONGRATULATIONS AND REGRETS

While extending congratulations, his parishioners will hear with regret that the Rev. J. M. Buckmaster will in November cease to be senior curate at the Warrington Parish Church and priest-in-charge of St. George's Mission in order to become Vicar of St. Andrew's, Wigan.

A son of the Rev. C. J. Buckmaster, vicar of Hindley, the reverend gentleman is not un- acquainted by any means with the district in which he is about to labour, having resided within view of it for many years prior to going to Keble College, Oxford. Although he will enter his new incumbency full of enthusiasm for his work, the reverend gentleman will not go away from Warrington without feelings of great regret. "It will be the biggest blow of my life leaving here," he told an " Examiner " interviewer. "I have always found the people easy to get on with and if I find them' the same elsewhere I shall hnve nothing to complain of. I am certain I shall never like any other people the same as these wherever I go. Perhaps it is that one always tikes to those in one's first parish. As for the way in which they have borne the sorrows of war, it is simply wonderful. They have been hard-hit, and if, before the war began, they had known what was going to happen they would never have stood it in the way they have' done. It has been simply marvellous, and I shall feel deeply the parting from them." In his work at St. George's, Mr. Bnckmaster has been heavily handicapped during the war by the enlistment of many of his best workers. Not a single Sunday school teacher has been left to him—the last one joined the Army on Monday—and three sidesmen and every chor- ister but one have also gone. Despite this, however, by dint of earnest service on his part and that of the workers left, the attendances both in the Mission and the Sunday school have been more than maintained. , His organist, Mr. Peter Hardman, enlisted on Monday and has been succeeded by the latter's cousin, Master-Ruben Knight, a twelve- year-old boy, who is Showing great, promise as a musician.

During his stay in Warrington, since Septem- ber, 1911, when he succeeded the Rev. T. J. Ridpath, Mr. Buckmaster has endeared him- self to all those with whom he has come-into contact. His popularity as a visitor ig great, and his sermons are characterised by thought-. fulneis and sincerity. Last (Friday) evening the Rev. .T. M. Buck- master, who has always been keenly interested a.nd helpful to soldiers enlisted from his parish, conducted the "ceremony of unveiling two pictures of the "Roll of Honour" in a framework on the wall of the Mission in Brick. street. Round the one picture—" The White Comrade"—lie the names of the men who have gone forth to war, and round the other—" The Great Sacrifice "—the names of the fallen.

VETERAN WHEELMEN.

Old-Time Cyclists' Rally At

Daresbury.

The official definition of an old-time cyclist describes him as "one who is over military age and who possessed and habitually rode an ' ordinary ' bicycle, solid or cushion-tyred ' safety ' bicycle or tricycle; or a person who has ridden a pneumatic-tyred bicycle or tricycle fora period of not less than 29 years."

The northern old-time /cyclists' rally at Daresbury on Sunday aroused a great deal of interest, and although the first event of the kind in the Nprth of England, representatives from the N.C.U., Liverpool and Manchester centres, Liverpool and Manchester C.T.C. and the Clarion and Anfield Club, Liverpool, 'at- tended in large numbers. Warrington was well represented, and Mr. James Critchley, who represents Warrington C.C. on the Liverpool Centre, N.C.17., and Mr. A. E. Sandbach (hon. organising secretary) were actively engaged during the afternoon selling pink badges as sou.venirs of the 'occasion, while white badges were sold to those who had tea, all the pro- ceeds being in aid of the Cyclists' Prisoners of War Fund. A good sum must have been realised, as the number of cyclists present is placed at 250. -

Memories of the early days of cycling were aroused by the old machines on view, and which were brought from Manchester in a motor lorry by Mf. Newsholnie, director of Messrs. Sutcliffe and Bingham, a great cycling enthusiast. ' These included a " bone-shaker " with wooden spokes and solid iron tyres; a "bantam," two "ordinaries," 57in. and 60in., and a " rational ordinary," with original pneumatic tyres. The velocipede, over half a century old, and built by Robert Muir, Victoria Bridge Works, Salford, was in a perfect state of preservation.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
A Prehistoric Feat.

The feat of .the day was performed by Mr. H. Dunning, who rode a 52in. machine—solid tyres —from Chester to Daresbury and back. The Spider, a Coventry -Challenge, made by Messrs. Singer and Co., had been laid up for 26 years until Sunday, but Mr. Dunning had it shining like a new pin. The members of the committee were Messrs. Edwin- Kite, chairman; W. P. Cook, Bob' Wilson, George Milne, Jos. Whalley, W. C. Cole; John E. Nagle, Alf. Sandbach (hon. secretary), Ted Battereby, W. Taylor, C. E. Pugh and T. Garrett.

A- large marquee was erected in the grounds of the Ring o' Bells, where tea was excellently served by Mr. Garnett, the proprietor. Mr. Edwin Kite, a veteran oiclist of half a cen- tury, presided, and the Rev. T. T. Lee Jones (vicar of Daresbury), who was an invited guest, thanked the company for their good wishes and for coming to the old-time village of Daresbury. As a cyclist of twenty years' standing, he wished them all suc- cess. A vote of thanks was passed to the com- mittee and Mr. Sandbach (hon. secretary). The National Anthem was 'sung and the company dispersed at six o'clock.

THE WAR BUDGET.

Writing on the subject, "How Peace Will Be Made," a well-informed contributor to the "War Budget describes in the current issue the formalities which will have to be observed in clinching the great world-bargain. Photographs of the signatures appended' to famous treaties illustrate the article. Edmund Dane discusses Germany's critical military position, and a popular contributor- describes the "fettling up" by which shilis are converted to naval use. The issue contains, among its pictorial features, the first of a series of naval cartoons by Lieutenant E. G. R. Beuttler, R.N.R., a nautical jester of striking artistic ability who has been called a naval Barnsfather.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 2, 1916.

CO-OPERATWE SOCIETY.

Growing Membership And Increasing Business,

===== 0002601_19160902_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
SCHOLARSHIP SCHEME.

The 223rd quarterly meeting of the Warring- ton Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society, Ltd., was held in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo- street, on Monday. .Mr. J. Upson presided over, a meeting including the —whole of the com- mittee and about sixty or seventy members.,

MR. UPSON ON THE WORK OF THE SOCIETY.

The President, moving the adoption of the report, referred to the case of an employe who had not been receivinc, any allowance from the Society, a question which had been raised at last quarterly meeting. It had been ascertained that, including the allowance to the man him- self, the dependents of this employe were in receipt Of a larger sum than he had been earn- ing while in the employ of the Society, and therefore no question of hardship or allowance could arise. It had been decided to allow Mrs. Davies to act as C.W.S. delegate in place of her husband, who had been called to the Colours.

The sales for the six months were £238,163, an increase of £27,460 over the corresponding period last year, and equal to an average purchase per member per week of Hs. 51d. The member- ship had increased by 365 and now stood at 16,017. He congratulated members on the assistance that had been given to the com- mittee by customers carrying away their own purchases. Over one hundred employes had enlisted. Two had been killed, and news had recently come that another one was missing, namely, R. Pepper, late employe in the drapery department, and the sympathy of ,the meeting was expressed to his wife. In accordance with the arrangements made at the previous quarterly meeting the sum of £4O 12s. 6d. had been paid to the dependents of the two men who had been killed, both of whcAu were single men. At the present moment the sum of over £l,OOO was being held in War Savings Certifi- cates for the benefit of the remainder of the single employes; the Government in the mean- time had the use of the money. The balance of surplus on the trading for the six months, after providing for the interim dividend of 3s. in the 2, already paid, enabled the committee to recom. mend again that 3s. should be paid on all pur- chases, including butchery. Although. the dividend was 2d. in the 2 less than a year ago, the members had bad the benefit of at least 4d., in the 2 in reduced prices, or in keeping down" the selling prices where cots have abnormally advanced. Recently, when in spite of the fazt that the cost of flour had advanced from 13s. to 16s. per sack, the selling price of the Society's bread had been actually reduced. Bread was being retailed at the present moment at 71d. pet 41b. loaf, which after deducting the 3s. divi- dend, was equal to not quite 60., and he con- sidered that nowhere in the country at the present time could bread be obtained more cheaply than from the Society. The action in- stead of being appreciated, brought a storm about their ears from the private trading in- terest; but in this particular instance owing to the foresight of the manager contracts for flour were placed before the advance, and they gave the members the benefit in the reduced prices of the main necessary of life. The adoption of the report was seconded by Mr. England, and supported by Mr. Cook. Both paid a tribute to the manager, Mr. Atkinson, for his keenness in buying, Mr. Cook considering that the time had arrived when the appreciation should be put into practical form by a recon- sideration of salary. The report was adopted and the balance sheet accepted. On the accounts of the Education Fund a member took exception to the charges for the " Wheatsheaf "; and Mr. W. Lowe, considering that the members were not being educated as they should be, suggested that the time had arrived when a special Education Committee apart from the members of the Board should be appointed.

Mr. Green Chappell, auditor, briefly referre4 to the sound financial position of the Society.

The members approved the suggestion.

EVENING CLASSES.

Students Realising The Benefit

OrA Good Education.

When they grow up into Misters the frisky Masters, who have recently left schc.ol and now spend their off-work hours purely in play and pleasure, will realise with a shock the advin- tages which their industrious compeers are enjoying through having obtained a good education.

It is highly satisfactory to observe that in regard to attendances at evening schools War- rington is usually well above the average, and it is expected that when the classes reopen on the 11th inst. those presenting themselves for admission will be even greater in number than ever before. Instead of the war affecting deleteriously the number of evening class students a distinct advance was .noted in the winters which have fallen during the time of crisis. Naturally, among the boys, however, the attendances were not as regular owing to the considerable amoun4, of overtime they were in- duced to work in the making of munitions of war. Many of the boys even appeared at their classes dusty-faced, having migrated there straight from work—and great credit to them, too. Despite this inability to attend as regu- larly as desired, it is a curious but gratifying fact that the examinations were quite up to the standard of last year, the keenness of .the boys and the teachers obtaining this result. Of course, the students at the evening schools are largely the children of better-c•-ous artisans. These parents seem to realise what they lack themselves and urge upon their boys the necessity of study as an •essential to success. Another factor who has increased the 139Pu- larity of evening schools ir the growing interest which employers are displaying in this im- portant phase of educational development, some of them going so far as to pay the fees of the boys, while others give prizes to those who pass the examinations.

Especially at this time a good education com- mends itself, for in the trade war on the con- clusion of military hostilities it will exercise a dominant part, and as many boys and girls as can sliould take advantage of the chance of de- veloping their abilities and acquiring much knowledge. That is why the evening pch6ols will reopen. As has been said, Warrington figures well above the average in evening class students, but, it must be added, there will be room for improvement as long as even one adolesceht spends his or her evenings in winter time without serious study. Classes for women are to be reopened at Evelyn-street, Bolton and Beamont Schools, where instruction will be given in dressmaking. cookery and millinery—all subjects of special interest to them. Although a considerable number of ladies usually present themselves, it is. hoped that this year they will be far greater, for the instruction given is of the utmost utility.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

A Scottish Factory for Educated Women.

Very few men, or women either, would have dared before the war to assert that engineering was a suitable profession for the so-called weaker sex. Munition work has brought about a change of opinion on this subject, and proved that the higher as well as the lower branches of the industry can be advantageously opened to women. So convinced is a Scottish manufac- turer of the value of women's work, as engi- neers, that he is about to invest a large sum in building an ideal factory, in which educated women only will be employed. Those with mechanical talent will here find permanent and well-paid work after the war is over. Another factory for munitions has undertaken to employ the widows of fallen officers, and to provide ex- ceptionally good accommodation for the ladies and their families in a healthy, bracing village. The work is by no means heavy, and the pay is good, but the hours are rather long. Women in Trades.

The Government has for some time made special efforts to provide employers with suit- able Women substitutes for the men who have been called to the Colours. Figures have lately been collected which prove that numbers of women are working at trades and occuPations which have no connection with the war, and there 'is every reason to hope' that the vital in- terests of the nation will not suffer an account of the withdrawal of so many men from the industrial world. In the grain miffing trade about eight thousand women are employed in weighing, packing, • trucking, and the fighter operations. Some 'millers have introduced smaller sacks •for flour; in order that women may handle them. Women have replaced the ovenmen in some biscuit factories and bakeries, where special muctinery has been introduc\ed for drawing' off the hot air. The great heat is tem- pered by eoveri;ig the stoves .with asbestos, and thus 'making it possible for women to work. No fewer' than twenty tho-nsand women are working in the breweries, and they are wanted in ever increasing numbers in the boot trade, which is again occupied with orders for millions of pairs of boots for the Russian Army. -

Warnings to Women Workers. Dr. Elizabeth Chesser, M. 8., is of opinion that nursing homes and hospitals will be filled with women suffering from neurasthenia after the war. Thousands of women and girls now doing a man's work will, she thinks, succumb to various nervous ailments, and become invalids, unless they make a duty of safeguarding. their health during the present period of stress avpd strain. While women can go for a long period without food, and resist such diseases as pneumonia, typhoid fever, and tuberculosis, and have a stronger grip on life than men, they are more unstable, and more easily deflected' from the normal. Dr. Chesser advocates good nourish- ing food for war workers of every, class, ten hours in bed every night, and systematic exercise out of doors, even for the woman who carries heavy weights all day long. Milk, eggs, bread and butter, and fruit should be preferred to cakes, mineral waters, pastry and tea, which form the meals of so many feminine workers.

The Passing of Fastenings. Fastenings at their present rate of disajijidiz. ance will soon be things of the past, for no one has either time or patience for the numerous hooks and eyes that dressmakers considered necessary to the success of a corsage three or four years ago. The newest nightdresses are slipped over the head, and have not even one button, the same remark applies to other gar- ments for day or night wear. Simplicity is the order of the day, and elaborate trimmings are considered in bad taste, . hence, perhaps, the resort to masculine pyjama nightwear. These, however, are being.slowly, but surely, ousted by the more feminine slumber suits, which have advantages not possessed by pyjamas. They admit of some adornment, such as smocking at the waist and neck, and a little beautifying by means of ribbons. They are very cool in hot weather, and nothing could be more comfortable and convenient when travelling than the slum- ber suit in Shantung, which is a favourite choice. Green Peas.

A quarter of an hour by the clock is the cor- rect time for one peek. The saucepan in whiith they are to be cooked should be put on with quart of freshly drawn cold water, three salt- spoonfuls of salt, a small piece of loaf sugar, and four or five sprays of mint. Over brisk heat the water will boil in ten minutes. The peas should be lifted in in large cupfuls, not all in one movement, but immediately succeeding each other, so that the water does not cease to boil. Put on lid, reduce the heat to a degree that keeps the contents merely simmering, and in exactly fifteen minutes they will be ready for turning into a hot vegetable dish. This is the way to cook young peas:. If they are, on the turn of middle age they will need twenty minutes. The secret of having green peas sent to table in perfection may be summed up in four words: From pod to pot. I have known cooks shell them early in the morning for late dinner. I have even known them to be shelled the previous day. This is a mistake. There should be no greater interval than is necessary for the process- of shelling: "From pod to pot." Peas lose succulence and flavour when long shelled. The water that is strained away from them should be put back in the saucepan, allowed to boil quickly with the lid off for ten minutes and is then an excellent foundation for soup.—" Gentlewoman."

Outdoor Sleeping. Sleeping outdoors—this is the most important thing a sufferer from tuberculosis can do. The outdoor sleeping habit did not spread rapidly at first; it has had to make its way against the prejudices of people who cling to ancient habits and customs. The pioneer in the outdoor sleep- ing movement was, so far as known, Doctor Millett, of Massachusetts. His discovery came about, t s /many great innovations do, by the pressure of necessity. He was treating as patients some workers in the Brockton shoe factories. Some years ago a man came to him for advice, and Doctor Millett said, "You must stay outdoors all day." "But," the man said. "I can't. I have to work." The doctor said, "You must give up work, you must rest." The man replied, "Then' I would starve, and my family would starve, too. I would rather die at work than be a burden to my family." Then Doctor Millett began thinking what he could do. He realised the necessity of the man stay- ing in Brockton and working, as he did, by an alcohol lamp in a vitiated atmosphere. So he put the man out on the roof to sleep, and in three months the patient was well.—" Daily Telegraph."

THE PRIZE EVENT.

By Nephew Joseph Perry, 83, Hoyle-street; Heathside School.

"I was on my holidays at ' -Lowton with my aunt. One afternoon an aero- plane going from - to , fell in the open fields, with its boiler burst. I am glad to say that not a person was hurt; even the soldier-driver of twenty-one years old was not hurt. Thousands of people came to see it. Policemen and soldiers came to guard it. The wings of the aeroplane were torn in three or foUr places. When teatime came they began to collect for the Red Cross Society, and any- one who wanted to go in the field where it was had to pay a penny. If they had started to collect when it dropped they would have had a nice sum of money by when it went dark. A few days later motor lurries came and carried it 'back to f f

HIGHLY COMMENDED.

Niece Lilian Himsworth, 15, Fitzherbert- street (Beamont), one of the oldest members of the Corner, whom we have missed very much' indeed, writes:—" You will think I am forget- ting you, but I have not done; I have been away a long time. I should say the best event was when I went to Greenhill, outside Sheffield. It was a lovely day. We went on the car first, and whez4 we got off we began to play ball on-the way, and we had not gone far when we knocked a big ball over a private wood. We resumed our journey and turned off to the fields, and here we could not play as part of the fields had vege- tables growing. On our way we watched• a cricket match, and then we got to Greenhill. We got tea soon after in a very nice cottage, and after we played rounders in the field be- hind. We stayed there about an hour and a half and then made our way home. We did not go back the same way that we came. We passed the beautiful old Beauchief Abbey, where the monks used to live. We got on the car, after walking through a lovely wood, and returned home a happy, but tired, band." Nephew Horace Himsworth, 15, Fitzherbert- street, says- the most interesting event in his holidays was a visit to Cordwell Valley, ,near Sheffield. He and a friend made the journey by car and on foot. "On the way home in the evening we saw a rabbit in a field; then we heard the bang of a gun." - Nephew Fred Rathbone, 112, Algernon-street (Parochial), visited, Winvick Delph with his two pals,. Denis Farrar and Harry Heywood, and a little boy, Teddy Yates, who fell and hurt his knee.

Nephew Harry Heywood, 8, Matthews-street (Parochial), says a visit to Appleton Dingle was the best event in his holidays. He and Denis Farrar rambled all over the Dingle, "as if we were after rabbits." They carved their names on a tree and then itarted cut to discover things. A brook runs through the Dingle, and when he was swinging on a tree he fell into the

water! Soon after they found two bottles and played at throwing them down the hill till they broke. Then they went to another part and got' lost. Nephew Harry has an eye for detail, and I think he will make a good writer some day.

Nephew Denis Farrar, 46, Willis-street (Paro- chial School).--" The most interesting event in my holidays was when I went to Appleton Dingle. As it was a hot day we took a flask cf water each; the journey •did not seem very long to me; still it was about three or four miles. I had a penny, so on my way I bought a pennyworth of biscuits. After the lady had served me she said in a faint voice, 'A good pennyworth'; and I should think it was; sure enough she had given me half a pound." Niece Lily McLachlan, 8, Ash-grove (Bolton Council School), went to New Brighton for a few days. On the journey she passed through some very interesting spots, but found the traffic in Liverpool a nuisance for those wishing to get to the Landing Stage. Crossing the river she saw a sunken fishing boat and some men engaged on rescue work.

Niece Emily Marshall, 183, Winwick-road.--- " When I went to Hereford we went to Lynton. From the top of Lynton Hill we looked right across the fields and saw the Malvern Rills, a lovely scene. A very beautiful sight was the river Wye. We •saw the town of Ross-on-Wye with its ancient but beautiful buildings. I saw May Hill, which cannot be measured because people don't know where it begins and where it ends. I went over the river Severn; in the river there are lovely salmon. The river Severn is compared with the Shannon. Altogether I saw two chief rivers, the Severn and the Wye."

Nephew Arthur Roundel!, 32, Gorsey-lane, thinks his best day was during the second week, when he went to spend a day at. New Brighton, mostly on the sands and paddling wt tile water. Niece Gladys Robinson, 61, Oldham-street (Bolton Council School), chooses a long walk in the country with her mother and sister. Sheep were brazing contentedly in the green fields; they seemed to enjoy the, sweet air and the lovely sunshine, for they were frisking about playfully. Further on were fieuts or golden wheat, glittering in the sunshine. How stately and proud it looked! But it would not be proud for long, for it was nearly ready for cutting. High up in the trees were birds chirping gaily. I enjoyed that day very much, and took much interest in the woods and trees.

Niece Edith Pugh, 17, Ship-street, Brecon, sends me a nicely written account of a visit to the waterfalls.

Niece Elsie Johnson (N 0.777 323, Lovely-lane (Wycliffe School).—" The most interesting object that I have seen during my holidays was a spring of clear, icy cold water. As it happened we were going to the mountains, and on our way we passed this spring, and it seemed as if it made a passage from the mountains and ran down this small opening in the wall, and it looked so tempting that we ran the risk of starv- ing our bodies for a few minutes. It was found to be as cold as ice and the most tempting and luscious water ever tasted."

Niece Fanny Osborne, 31, Leicester-street (Evelyn-street School), says that her mother took her to New Brighton, and it interested her very much to see what means they had pro- vided'to protect Liverpool from enemy boats and aircraft. "I also saw the divers trying to raise a ship that had been sunk in the river. It was interesting to watch them going under the water and coming up , again. I have been to New Brighton many a time, but have never seen so many people there before. It is a very nice place since they have made the new promenade. I saw a large number of wounded soldiers there, and I should think the fresh air .and sea breezes would do them good." Niece Edna Fellows, 44, Bewsey-road (Evelyn- street School).—" The most interesting event of my holidays was when I went to 'Red Rock.' I cannot tell you the reason why the name is 'Red Rock,' for there are no rocks near the place. It is a pretty village, surrounded by the prettiest country possible. It .is near Arley Woods. We went on the train, and then rambled through the woods. Afterwards we got some tea at a neighbouring farm. Then we took a look round the farm. There were several pigs. a lot of fowl, two guinea pigs, and horses and cows. Altogether it was an ideal farm. Every- thing • just like we read in story-books. After wandering round for a while, we plucked some lone stiff grasses, which grew in abundance everywhere, for my grandma's vases. We then returned to 'Red Rock,' and very soon we were speedily conveyed 'homewards, after spending a delightful and interesting time in the woods." Nephew Arthur Ford, 18, Portland-street (St. Alban's), went to Prestatyn and was in the sea every day. It was very good of the people of Warrington to do such a kind act.

FOOD OF A SORT FOR GERIVIANY.

Great. s.trides hav been made this week towards the achievement of that complete vic- tory upon which the Allies have 'fixed their determination and set their hearts. To begin with, Italy declared war upon . Germany and so gave her final ax4l irrevocable pledge that she will 'stand by the cause of progress and right to the end. But this dramatic move was immediately overshadowed by the action of Roumania. This proud, if little, nation declared war upon AUstria-Hungary and so threw in her lot with us and staked her for- tunes upon the probabilities .of our success. The Roumanians have a rich prize in store to repay them for their intervention. In their own land they Cumber only 7,000,000 people; beyond 'their borders they number quite an- other 7,000,000. In Transylvania; which the Magyars hold and where the Roumanians are ruthlessly oppressed, there are at least 4,000,000 Roumanians to be re-claimed and given a chance of living their own national life and speaking their own language, which is now denied them. So Much for what the Itoumaniang seek to get. What is it they can give? • A flood of first- rate reserves at the crisis of the war when reserves can be used to the be 4 advantage and when the enemy's reserves are believed to be well-nigh, if not completely, exhausted. They have forced the addition of 500 miles of line to the long stretch that has to be watched by Germany against an increasingly powerful foe watching to break in at the least sign of weakness. Threequarters of a million fresh and highly trained men, with nearly 2,000 well-equipped -guns, are a welcome addition to our forces, and open up a terrible pros- pect to our enemies. Austria is being hit heavily by Russia and Italy; now Roumania will take her at another point, and from reports received up to last night had suc- ceeded in making a material impression. The. land of our latest Allies has been thrown open to the Russians, who are thus early upon the journey to Bulgaria to avenge the renegade act of a year ago that did more than anything else to prolong the war. The Roumanian harvest will be available for us and withdrawn from our enemies, and, unless Hungary is very lucky, the harvests of her plains will be destroyed, or come our way. It is said that the food question is becoming acute in Germany. However that may be, the people of that unhappy country have this week had a plentiful supply pf that figurative species of food which is sometimes offered to us for reflection. Up to now, those in high places in Germany have shown their- dupes the map and fed them on delusive hopes of final victory. But if they. think at all the people of Germany must see what a serious portent the considered act of • Roumania is for them. Would the Roumanians, they must be _ _ _ _

_Asking themselves, have risked the fate of -Servia had not Germany lost the power of in- flicting it? Even in high places the, coming disaster casts its shadows before. Hinden- burg has been called, in to supersede Falken- hayn as Chief of the German General Staff. The Kaiser is thus caught in the act not only of swopping his horse while - crossing the stream, but of swopping at a moment when the stream is. exceedingly turbulent. There are theories that the advisers of the " All Highest' know that the 'time has come for the German hosts to contract their lines, and that the only possible contraction for the moment is on the Western front. A retreat in Northern France and Belgium would bring down the structure of Berlin bluff about the Kaiser's ears, and so the great Hindenburg is brought ,in to wake the necessary strategical movement to the rear and cover the disaster with the glamour of his name. That Roumania has recognised that the Central Powers are crumbling, and has con- veyed the news of that interesting fact to the world in the most practical-manner, is of even greater value at the present time than the admi4tedly valuable military assistance she is
