GOSSIP.

Are Special Constables always gentlemen?

In Shaw's " Pygmalion " there is a, scene in which a certain Professor of Phonetics, taking notes of conversation in a tramway shelter, is an object of curiosity by certain of the Cockneys. " He's a 'cop,' he is," said one. " From Scotland Yard, I bet," augments another "No, he ain't a 'cop.' He's a' gentle- man. Look at his boots !"—"Beautifully polished land shaped"—exclaims another, hitting the right -nail on the head.

The incident occurred to me on Sunday after- noon during the departure of the South Lancashire soldiers-from the town. Outside of Bank Quay Station the crowd were so eager to get near that they overwhelmed the "Specials," sweeping them on one side almost as lightly as if they had been skittles, treading on their toes without apologising, and making a steady flow of the soldiers into the booking hall imposiible.

Afterwards I noticed the :3ootsiof some ot' the

"Specials." The tops of them were dusty, marked ,severely where members of the crowd had caught them, and looking as unkempt as if they had been slouching around a forge all day —and Sunday afternoon, too.

Therefore, one asks: Were the " Specials ' gCntlenien or only 'cops '? Both, of ,course.

Warrington as a health resort. An Irishman at the Borough Police Court on Thursday said he came to England for the benefit of his health and started work as a labourer for ISt essrs. Pear- son and Knowles. The magistrates deciaed that he shall complete the "- cure " in the Army.

Next to the Recreation Room in. Bold-street and the privilege to ride free in our tramearS, I think cur wounded soldiers appreciate most highly the seats which have recently been placed for them near the tram terminus at the Central Station.

Noticing, like the rest of us, that our sick men in red, white and blue have often to wiit many minutes fora tramcar to Longford, Miss Heaton, of the Central Station Drive, unlike the rest of us, suggested to the Town Clerk that if he would have a couple of forms placed there she would send another.

In response the Town Clerk has sent three all labelled "For wounded soldiers only?' "My father, the late 'Alderman Heaton, used to say that he would rather walk 10 miles than stand half an hour," Miss Heaton remarked to me yesterday when I culled on her. "And that was what made me suggest the idea."

The seats are appreciated beyond measure, and it is a stirring sight to observe that when they are fully occupied and , a severely wounded warrior comes along one of the other fellows, who can better manage to stand, making way for his more unfortunate comrade.

Full of honour, another Warrington cen- tenarian has just paSsed away, and tribute is paid him in another column.

It is not out of place here to state that a stone in the Parish Churchyard dilates most quaintly on ages of the dead in the following verse:— Our life is but a winter's 'day,. • Some only breakfast and away, Others to dinner stay and are full-fed. • The oldest man but sups and goes to bed. Long is his debt who lingers out the day, . Who goes the soonest has the least fo pay. William Ilobey, died Septem%er 9th, 1777, aged 37 years. -

Shouldn't the .Christian name of the deceased be George?

Owing largely to the incleas2d cost of material and the adyanced selling price, the expenditure and the receipts of the electricity department Were up Materially last year.

The income increased from £25,419 to 1:34,403 and the expenditure from ...U.6,858 to £24,213, the gross profit going from £8,561 to £10,190. Charges on loans; &v., advanced from .R. 6,742 to £7,795, so that the net profit rose from 1:1,819 to £2,395.

If the price for current had not been raised last summer there would have been a loss on the year's working. Last year £4,893 of the debt was extinguished, leaving the outstanding loans at £79,752.

The General Purposes Committee have asked that sub-committees of the various committees having the control of workmen be appointed to re-consider the question of bonus paid to the employes of the Corporation.

MEN OF MILITARY AGE.

The Chief Constable And The

Dangers Of Employing Them.

The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) has this week addressed the following letter to the employers of the town:—

" Central Police Office, Warrington, August 28th, 1916.

"Dear Sirs,—Employment of men of military age. In consequence of cases which have this week come to the knowledge of the police where men of military age, who ought to have re- ported for service, were being retained in regu- lar employment locally, I have thought it well to call your attention to the provisions of the law in this respect, and to the notices regarding the same which have been widely displayed all over , the district.

"Briefly, it is an offence, punishable on sum- mary conviction, to employ a man of military age, who is not exempt from service or medi- cally unfit. Every man taken into, or con- tinued in employment, who is not badged, should be) required to produce card or.certificate showing he is exempt. "The onus of proof as to knowledge whether a man is of failitary age and liable to service or not is upon .the 'person employing him, and therefore it 'Would be well for employers, in their own interests, not to accept a man's mere statement that he is not liable for service, but to require definite proof before employing him. "In all cases where there is the slightest doubt it is most advisable to make inquiry of the Recruiting Authorities.—Yours faithfully, MARTIN NicHoms, Chief Constable."

===== 0002601_19160902_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
Belgian Refugee Home at Latchford.

The Belgian Refugee Home, Black Bear House, in connection with the Latchford' Baptist Church, has been closed. The accounts have been audited by Mr. Jas. Ball, certifying a balance in 'hand of £3O 4s. 4d. The total amount collected for the home has been £283 2s. 6d., which speaks well for practical sym- pathy to a troubled people. The balance is being divided between the Local Refugees /Fund, National Fund for Belgians in Belgium and interned British soldiers in Germany. Mr. Samuel Albinson, Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath, was the hon. treasurer of the fund.

TO PREVENT WAR IN FUTURE.

, Proposed International Tribunal To Rule The World.

ALTERNATIVES TO COMPULSORY SERVICE OR TRAINING.

By SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD, Bart.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Whether the remaining period of the war be long or short, its' duration has unques- tionably been greatly curtailed by that all-. important and happy event, 'the entry Of Roumania into the European battlefield. And it will probably not be very long. now before there is, increasing, and increasingly interest- ing speculation on different issues as to the post-war policies best calculated to serve the interests of the Empire. She settlement of large numbers of our returning soldiers and sailors upon the land under reasonably and sufficiently attractive conditions, and our commercial and fiscal- questions, are already assured oft a prominent part in these discussions. Also it is to be hoped questions of education, urgent hygienic problems, and several other matters; and the fact that we at present have for the period of the war a system of compulsory military service naturally raises the question as to whether this should' continue after the war, or as an alternative we should have, not compulsory military service, but compulsory military training; or again whether it would be advisable in military . matters to revert to a purely voluntary systc.m. That sge.in leads us Gn to the great inter- im tional .inestian as to whether the old system of competitive armaments is to be revived at the conclusion of the war; or whether it would obviously be to the advantage of the' Allied nations and of neutrals to put a stop to it. I propose in this letter to consider the last question and the bearing it may have upon naval and military problems at the conclusion of the war. .The references by leading statesmen, both Liberal and Conservative—like the Foreign Secretary and Earl Curzon of Kedleston—to the absolute necessity which. has now arisen for taking steps to prevent another world conflagra- tion are, in this connection of the highest significance. Is the great project they fore- shadow an impossible and -Utopian phantasy; or is it capable of being realised .as a practicable fact of the highest im- portance and greatest benefit to the civilised nations of the world? , Many of •us hold the latter view, and hold it Very strongly. Let us see how the matter stands: -

INTER-STATE AUTHORITY BACKED UP BY UNITED INTER-STATE FORCE.

That questions of conflicting interest will arise between different nations in- the futufe—as in the past-7--is not merely a matter of probability, but of certainty. Whether the matters in dis- pute concern questions of territory, immigra-4 tion, fiscal relations, or what not, the all-im- portant object is to get them settled by arbitra- tion before an International Tribunal. Whether such a Tribunal be held at the Hague, Or what- ever other venue might be chosen—a mere mater of detail—we must see to it that there is such a backing of intePriational naval and military force as will make the decrees of such a Tribunal absolutely authoritative. ' Now, if ever an international settlement on these lines is to be achieved, the conclusion of the present war will be, par excellence, the op- portunity to bring it into force. In that case, and securely to pave the way to it, the greatest. Powers would no doubt come to an understand- ing, of a permanent character. Take the far-reaching territorial questions concerning spheres of influence in Persia which concern Rvssia and ourselves. We should have to come to an agreement that would prevent any dan- gerous rivalry between the British and Russian Empires in that quarter, and there is obviously ' nothing in the world to prevent us coming to a permanent understanding With Russia wherever her spheres of influence march with ours, or there is a buffer State like Afghanistan that separates the two Empires. Other questions cOncerning the East, of vast and far-reaching importance no doubt in the future, should still come Osolutely within the sphere of control of the International Tribunal.

Broadly speaking, there is no territorial ques- tion looming ahead that should for one moment be allowed to prevent a permanent and satis- factory understanding between the British and .Russian Empires. Nor is there any such ques- tion that should ever create difficulties between France and ourselves. There can be no doubt that the rapprochement between .France and England is not at all of a temporary character, but is destined to have the highest and most permanent political significance. For similar reasons it is certain that all the -tre- mendous experiences of these years tend to strengthen and perpetuate relations between Italy and England, which have for long enough now been of a very cordial character. France and Russia are firm friends and Allies of long standing; and as regards Ffance and Italy, there is no conceivable question that might be at issue between them in the fitture which would make another outbreak of war anything but the act of mad men. We have just been celebrating the centenary Of peaceful relations between Great Britain and the United States of America. More than one difficult, and even dangerous, question has been dealt with in that period and settled by arbitration, and even in pre-war days the statesmen of both nations were fast ap- proaching, if they had not already reached, the conviction that the settlement of disputes be- tween the two nations by an appeal to arms was for all time intolerable; in fact; a disaster to both nations and a crime against civilisation not to be thought of. The European war is cer- thin to accelerate and strengthen such convic- tions. If there were people in years gone by who thought that Canada in the .natural course of things would gravitate to the United States, the overwhelming majority of level-headed Americans realised clearly enough that the union of Canada with the United States against the will of the Canadians would be utterly im- practicable and preposterous; while, on the other hand, the splendid loyalty of Canadians to the Empire, evident enough before the war, has during the course of the war been magnifi- cently manifested before the whole world. With that great fact accepted as a starting point, all other issues that may arise in the future between Britain and the United States should, and—who can doubt it?—unquestionably will, as hereto- fore, be settled by arbitratioit.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT WORTH ANOTHER WORLD WAR.

Now, of course, some very difficult questions are going to present themselves immediately after this war; for instance, the future of the Turk and of Asia Minor—extremely difficult problems for the Powers to deal with; and, in the More distant future, one can imagine ques- tions arising in connection with South America of a very delicate and awkward character: But none of these problems, either in the near or more distant future, are worth another world war; and what has fallen from our own states- men of both parties recently, and from the President of the United States, clearly points to steps being taken such as will make a second Armageddon impossible. Such steps are, in fact, a matter of sheer necessity unless, bent on playing the part of Kilkenny cats, the nations are in the future to take leave of their senses. Let' us at any rate assume or the sake of argument that such steps' will be taken at the close of this war. If that is done the great majority, or let us hope all `the neutral Powers, including the United States of America,, would join forces with the present. Allies, and those forces would give an irresistible sanction to the decrees of the Supreme Court which all these Powers would then establish. 'Once that Court had come into existence it would be such a Godsend to the civilised world that there would not be the slightest chance of it ever i being dissolved. At its nception, and as an essential condition of the very existence of such a Council, there would necessarily be an agree- ment as to the proportions of naval and mili- tary forces to be maintained by the. different Powers. Such a comprehensive settlement of the question of aviiamentB would take the place of the system of competitive armaments, and is indeed the only satisfactory and permanent alternative to that system; and it would natur-

"A GREAT PUSH."

Forward Movement By Church Of England.

WHEN PEACE WILL COME.

"In the future the 9hurch is going to be judged by the way it brings ,forth fruit, by the way it 'delivers the goods," was the striking declaration of the Rev. Canon Goudge, the Archbishops' Messenger, who attended a meet- ing of churchworkers at the Patten Hall, Win- marleigh-street, on Thursday evening, in con- nection with th 4 National Mission. The reverend Canon believes that the success of the National Mission is essential to peace. It was not much good us wishing that the war was going to end just because we wanted it to, he said. It would ,not end before it had done its work—that of bringing people back to God. What we had to do was to follow the method of Elijah during the three years' fimine by corporately and individually bear- ing our witnesses to God. We must call the individual, the Church, and the nation to re- pentance, and when that had been done we could call to God to end the war. There had got to be a full repentance in our own per- sonal lives, in our Church life, and in our national life. Each one had to repent, not just the jailers and the penitentiaries, for repentance was like charity—it should begin at home. It wa,s not just for the vicar and his wife and the curate's wife. Is It A True Bill ?

Against England the charge was made of callousness, the giving up of prayers, mate- rialism, love of pleasure, intemperance, and lust. Before we ourselves could charge England with those sins we held got to ask ourselves whether we were guilty of. them, too. If so, we must repent and alter our conduct. The National Mission which is shortly to take place was meant to be "A Great Push," with the object of making religion in England very different from what it .was. The reason we needed this great revival of religion was that for many years, not only in England, but on the Continent of Europe, we had been going from bad to worse. The alteration- of that state of affairs, he pointed out to that meeting of churchworkers, depended mainly upon them. The clergy, no doubt, would have their own part to take by guiding and inspiring them, but, after all, the success of the Mission would devolve upon them. In the Church there must be a wonderful spirit of social brotherhood. Men baust he attracted from the social' life of the public-house to the social life of a far higher standard to be found .in the Church. By their example and enthusiasm, by "deliv- ering the goods," as it were, churchworkers must lead England back to God.

The Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) presided over a large audience, which included clergy and members of the Established Church from all over the district.: Throughout the meet- ing, which began with evening prayer, a spirit of reverence and enthusiasm for the forthcom- ing Mission was manifested, and the thanks of the audience for the inspiration given by the Archbishops' Messenger was voiced by the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross and Mr. J. V. Partington.

GONE TO RUSSIA.

Miss Lucy Broadbent With Scottish Hospital Unit.

Miss Lucy Broadbent has this week sailed for Archangel en route to Odessa with the Bcottish Women's Hospital Unit, which has. undertaken and been appointed by the Russian Government to serve with an army of Serbians, who, 'being forced to fight with the Austrians at the beginning of the war, were taken pri- soners by the Russians and now have been re- constituted by them. The Scottish women have done much excellent work, having provided units for Serbia and France. They provide complete field hospitals, the whole personnelle being women—doctors, nurses, orderlies, ambu- lance and transport drivers, &c. Miss Lucy Broadbent has taken the past of laundry superintendent.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPULSION-IN SECONDARY EDUCA- TION.

For years past Germany has been forcing the pace by increasing her naval and military ex- penditure, and well-founded suspicion of these preparations has led other Powers to do the same. There will be no reason whatever why this vast increase of expenditure during recent years upon competitive armaments should not be cancelled at the conclusion of the present war. If that were done it would bring about a condition of "as you were" all round. It would still leave us with our unchallenged and unchallengeable sup-iemacy on the water, and our regular army for over-sea service as well. We might stipulate for some hundreds of thousands of Territorials in addition beyond that, but if competition in armaments is at length to be checked, could we in common fair- ness to other Allied Powers demand more than this? If we did so, if in addition to the great superiority of our naval power, which is a sine qua non for our Empire, and to the other mili- tary forces above mentioned we demanded the right to have every able-bodied man in the country trained to arms as well, should we not lay ourselves out to provoke the suspicions of our neighbours? Again, if without compulsory training for everyone we are able by voluntary service to get all the naval and military power we require from the point of view of national and imperial safety, and to satisfy the require- ments of the International Supreme Court, would it from an exclusively British point of view be to the advantage of Englishmen, Scotsmen and Welshmen to institute compulsory military training for every able-bodied lad? No doubt it is strongly in our interests that compulsion in one form or another should continue to regu- late a lad's life after he has reached the age of fourteen. Attendance at night school, or techni- cal training in the career a lad has chosen, might well be compulsory, and in many cases it might be advisable to make it obligatory on employers to give opportunities for such in- struction; but from a physical point of view there are other forms of comptlsory training which would do more for the physique of the nation than military drill.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTARY SOLDIER BETTER THAN

CONSCRI PT.

Previous to the war many people were under the impression that as between the German Nation, which had compulsory military train" ing, and the British Nation, which had not that advantage, it would certainly be found when it came to the pinch that the German had most grit in him, and was, from a fighting point of view, the better man of the two. This war has clearly shown that that is not the case. It has done more: it has shown that man for man the average Briton has been a good deal more than a match in the field for the average Ger- man; and it has on an enormous scale demon- strated that superiority of the voluntary soldier to the conscript, which was impressed upon the authorities in England many a generation since. Well, we have that advantage, and we can get all the soldiers and sailors we need to serve the old country and the Empire, and to assist in safeguarding the peace of -the world. we can get them all on voluntary lines. Why should we throw that advantage away? I do not anticipate that for one moment we ;shall be asked ,to do so. The issue will rather arise in connection with •com- pulsory or voluntary training, and on that point I think we may still fairly ask the advocates of compulsory training whether they think the compulsory principle so desirable in itself that we should adopt it, even if the regulation of armaments by international negotiation be- comes, after this war, an accomplished fact, and if a voluntary system would furnish all, and more than all the forces we should require for the different branches of the services under such a regenerated international regime.

Yours Sincerely, ARTHUR CROSFIELD.

41, West Hill, Highgate, London, N,

Whispering in Church.

" It'is tempting," says the September number of "St. James' Parish Magazine," "if one arrives in church in very good time, to whisper to one's neighbour about many things—as, for example, who is going to be the preacher ?—ivhat is the selection of hymns?—and besides there is always some bit of information to impart to or elicit from a kindly neighbour. Such de- sires, though not unnatural, should be stetnli repressed. Whispering is more audible than people generally suppose, and it disturbs those who wish to be perfectly quiet—as they should be—in God's House. The writer of this article had the pleasant experience a short time ago t° attend the evening service in a. Qinall church at the foot of the Cumberland hills, arriving about a quarter of an hour before even- song began. There was a very good congregt" tion and their behaviour was admirable; there was no whispering or semi-audible conversation. It was evident that they had 'learnt to observe the custom of maintaining silence in God'9 House."

MAYOR SEES FAIR-PLAY.

One eye on our front page and the other on your counterfoil and you will soon know whe- ther you are one of the winners of the 200 prizes in connection with the monster draw in aid of the Nrayor of Warrington's Fund for the purchase of food and comforts for prison- er s of war of the South Lancashire Regiment fl Germany.

• Out of 100,000 tickets issued 60,000 were sold, :and as a tesult the fund is expected to'benefit by about £5BO. Purchasers of tickets are not zaerely confined to Warrington and its suburbs, Widnes and St. Helens—the homes of many 'South Lancashire Regimental soldiers who are .prisoners of war—scoeping in a great number. .T6O worth was sold at Widnes. It was on Wednesday evening in the Parr Rail that the fate of the tiaket-holders was sealed. To witness the draw about 150 invita- tions to prominent citizens and helpers had been sent out, the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) presiding, and, as he said, seeing-that fair-play was given to all. _

The Mayor -having unlocked the tin ;ballot boxes, the counterfoils were then poured on to the platform, round half of which wire- netting had been erected so as to prevent any of the many-coloured papers slipping away, and also to isolate them. The honour of standing inside the netting (like a prisoner of :War) and picking up the tickets at random as allocated to Master Frank Pemberton, a bright little fellow of six years, who enjoyed the task as much as Miss Mary Gaulter, 2r°linger by two years, who, stationed on the other side of the netting next to the Mayor, extracted the numbers of the prizes- simultan- "llBlY from a glass biscuit barrel. The results :were then chalked on blackboards. On a table on the platform lay the 200 prizes under discussion. Sparkling in their ornamen- tation or looking stolid and proud in their use- fulness, they seemed to say "Whose shall I i?e?" On an average they were. worth 10s. each, but, of course, some were Much more valuable—notably four .R. 5 Exchequer Bonds. Su patriotic and generous are the tradesmen and inhabitants of the town and district that little difficulty was experienced in gathering them in, Mr. T. H. SpinkS and Mr. J. H. Gittens only needing to devote ten hours to obtain them.

OUR DUTY.

During the evening the Mayor addressed the audience upon the necessity for looking after °or prisoners of War. He did not think it was necessary to emphasise that much further, be said. It was admitted in the House of Commons, not long ago, by Mr. Tennant (who lv,as then Under-Secretary of State for War) that "had it not been for the parcels sent from tngland many of the prisoners would have starved to death." Still more recently, an exchanged prisoner, who called at the Parr Ilan to render thanks for the assistance which bad been sent him during his captivity, stated that "the parcels saved their lives." He said that nothing in the parcels was wasted. It !tad been their custom to save all the soft food, ke "Force," crushed oats, &c., in order to -Rave some invalid food to fall back upon in ease any of them fell ill. The way in which the German authorities were ill-treating and Starving the prisoners was, of course, indefen- sible. Nevertheless, our immediate duty was to see that these gallant men, who risked their all for us, did not suffer while we discussed reprisals, and waited for the day of reckoning, Which was so sure to come. It was for us to Iflake certain the supply of comforts for our Prisoners, and for this a steady supply of raoney was needed. As Warrington was the depot town of the South Lancashire Regiment, the burden of caring for that regiment rested largely upon them. The task was no light one, and Warrington was not a very large area from which to seek the wherewithal for so great an effort. The list of subscriptions, as pub- lished each week in the local Press, had for some time been short of the requirements, hence their effort that night. Those who had given to it, and those who had worked for it, Would be cheered to know that they hoped that the fund would benefit by it to the tune Of about 1.580, a sum which was urgently needed to carry on the work through the Winter months.

COL. MOGGRIDGE THANKS EVERYBODY. Colonel Moggridge said that, in addition to tile Mayor, their thanks were due to the very nlanY ladies and gentlemen wit° had assisted with this effort and with the humdrum work 0f the fund all the year round. First of all, to the lady helpers, most of whom had for ever twelve months packed the parcels each IVednesday; the proprietors of entertainment halls, who had kindly given them free adver- ,tisements of their needs in their " houses"; tu the large number of ladies and gentlemen from various large works, and others, who had assisted in the folding of the counterfoils; to tile generous prize-givers, who made the draw Possible; to the sellers and to the purchasers of tickets; and finally to those who organised Ihe draw—chiefly the staff at Parr Hall, par- ticularly Mr. Gaulter, and to Mr. J. H. Oittens and Mr. H. R. Hope, not forgetting t,.}l:e scrutineers, Mr. Bibby, Mr. Evans and 41-r• Houghton.. A prize for the gentleman 'Selling the greitest number of tickets was given by Messrs. T. & J. L Tunstall & Co., ,aud one for the lady selling the largest num- °et' by Mrs. Lindsay, of Sale. The ladies' Prize has been won by Mrs. Wyman, of Wid- nes, Mrs. Spinks being the runner-up. The gentlemen's prize fell to Mr. Archibald Brown. Thanks to the kindness, of the winners both these prizes had been given back into the filnd. 'll3 all the above ladies and gentlemen the thanks of the officers and men of the South -Lancashire Regiment, and particularly those Of them who were in captivity, were due and Were heartily accorded. The work of looking after the South Lancashire Regiment's pri- soners was a heavy one, he added. The second battalion was in action at the very beginning of the war, and, being in action against superior numbers, although -not superior valour, bore a rather heavy punish- kent, consequently its prisoner list was well above the average size. An expenditure of ever .230 per week had been sustained by the Mayor's Fund for many months, and it had new been found necessary to organise this special effort to counteract the decline in sub- scriptions which has been felt for some time Past.

RETURNED PRISONER'S TRIBUTE.

"The parcels saved our lives—keep on this goad work." This phrase is familiar to the Illajority of people in Warrington. The words uleet our view at Market Gate every day, and ~ver3' evening they speak out from the screens various places of amusement. They have 2,erved most effectively in keeping before us 'lle pressing needs of our poor fellows who Are Drisoners of war in Germany; and should urge ev„erYone to continue the good work. R.:01 e author of these striking words is "Crgeant James, who may be heard of at The -ae.tount, Walton-road, Stockton Heath. If zergeant James' life depended on the parcels food-stuffs sent out from home, they must ave arrived very regularly as far ,as he is elineerned. He is a fine, sturdy specimen of Plasculinity and has apparently fully recovered Nil the effects of any privations he may have suffered. t,lnberviewed by a representative of "The -I.3arainer," Sergeant James courteously but ly refused to give any account whatever as 2,114; own experiences and only, smiled when. d if he had escaped from Germany, and, gldnot, how he was so lucky as to get back to .... England. "I have my diary," he said, and can tell something after the war." At Divsent he said he was only anxious about the °l' fellows in Germany and -could only re- again and again "Beep sending out Parcels."

LCE.-CPL. A. KENWRIGHT.

One Of A Patriotic Family Missing.

Mrs. Ellison, Orford-lane, would welcome any news of her brother, Lance-Corporal Albert Kenwright, of the King's (Liverpool) Regi- ment, who was posted as missing after an engagement on July 27th. Thirty-one years of age, Lance-Corporal Kenwright completed seven .years i service in. India about five months before the outbreak- of war, when he was called up as a Reservist.. An "old boy" of the Silver-street School, he was 4mployed for about five months by Messrs.. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. Two brothers, a brother-in-law and three nephews are with the colours, all the nephews having been wounded.

PRIVATE J. SANKEY.

Buried In A Little Cemetery Near To Where He Fell.

Private James Sankey, of the Royal Lancast,ers, who resided with Mrs. Richardson at 108, Lord Nelson-street, is officially reported to have been killed on August 10th. He joined the Army in January of this year. Writing to Mrs. Richardson, the Rev. J. M. Watson says: "I buried your brother in a little cemetery near the place where he was killed., Doubtless you have heard already from his platoon officer how he gave his life for his country. A cross has been erected over his grave by the battalion." Private Sankey had been employed by Messrs. R. A. Naylor, Ltd., and latterly with the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd. He was educated at the National Schools, and was 23 years of age.

KILLED WHILE RESCUING TIIEIII OFFICER.

How Private Chandler Died.

A few 'weeks ago we recokded the death in action of Private Erank Chandler, of tne South Lancashire Regiment, son of Mr. Wm. H. Chandler, of Manchester, and brother to Mr. Wm. H. Chandler, 151, Orford-lane, Warrington. The widow of the gallant soldier has received a letter from the British Red Cross Offices in London, in which it is clearly indicated that he died a real hero's death. The letter states : " Dear Madam,—Since you inquired of us about your husband, I regret to learn that the War Office has officially informed you of his death, and I beg to send you my sincere sym- pathy. "I think you may like to receive the follow- ing account of the great gallantry and self- devotion which caused your husband's death. Pte. John McGrath (now in Mill-road Hospital, Liverpool) tells the following story At Lieut. Beavan was seen lying wounded outside, and Pte. Chandler called for volunteers to get him in. Three men went with him, and Pte. Chandler gave Lieut. Beavan first aid. Then the four men tried to get the officer in on their rifles, as there was no stretcher, but just as they reached the trench a shell struck them. It

is baldly likely now. .. . It will be a source of comfort to you to know that your son died like a man at the high call of duty to country, King and God."

===== 0002601_19160902_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPTAIN'S TRIBUTE.

The Rittman's Company Commander; Captain R. S. Stafford, has also written:— " On the afternoon of July 27th, the battalion had been attacked and taken Wood," he states. , " The Germans made many strong but unsuccessful counter attacks. It was during one of these that your son was killed. His platoon was on the extrozae right of the battalion in a very important position. It was a specially hostile attack by superior numbers, but, despite losing all but eight Men, they kept off the enemy—a very fine performance. Your son was a good soldier, and I know his platoon officer, who was also killed that day, had a very opinion of him. It may give you some consolation to know that he was killed in a splendidly suc- cessful action, and not, as often happen, in ordinary trench warfare, so tlmt he did not give Ws life in vain."

HOW HORACE BRIMELOW DIED.

"At The High Call Of Duty To Country, King And God."

Particulars, as to how Rifleman Horace Brimelow met his death are 'afforded in a letter of sympathy. which his chaplain,. the Rev. A. G. Dutton, has written to hissparents, Mr. and Mrs. G. L. Brimelow, of Manchester-road.„

"From what I have been able to hear from members of his platoon," he states, "he If as one of those - who went over the top in

Wood ilnd had not gone far when he was wounded. After that his friends who knew him were separated from him by having to go on, and, I understand, having been dressed by a. comrade, your son was returning to an, Aid Post. 'Unfortunately, the concentrated artillery of the 'enemy made the return jour- ney, to safety a very hazardous one. Many latis were caught in the barrage fire, and -some actually in the motor ambulances met their death. It seems most probable that your son was killed by 'shell fire. As the Brigade was shortly afterwards taken out of the Wood they were unable to bury all the dead. It is just possible you may hear more definite news of his being buried 'from one of the parties who went up for .that purpose, although I war that that

credit this statement and is very properly full of hope, as there are so many possibilities to consider. She has written to the War Office explaining the circumstances, and has received a reply stating that full inquiry would be made.

Private Churehill, who is 25 years of 9,ge, had always a great desire to join the Navy, and tried on two or three occasions to do 609 but was rejected owing to a slight accident to one of his toes. At the beginning of the war— in fact, three weeks after it broke out—he enlisted and joined the K. 0.5.8. He is quite a veteran in war work, having fought at Hill 60. He was wounded last May, having the index finger of his right hand blown off. He was "gassed" twice last year, and on getting better was set to it again and threw bombs with his left hand. A fine, courageous fellow, his letters home were always bright and light- hearted. • , Private Churchill was reared and educated in Stockton Heath and was formerly employed in the Spade Works as a stamper.

Wounded In Four -Places. Etrit Progressing.

• Private Frank Evans, of the South. Lancs. Regiment, only son of Mr. an Mrs. G. E. Evans; 108, Wellfield-street,, has fulen, wounded and brought to hospital at Glasgow. From there he writes to his two sisters as follows:— "You need not worry about my wounds. I am wounded in four• places—right shoulder, right buttock,, left thigh, and kit foot, so think I ..have had my whick, haven't I? I was wounded on the morning oif August Bth about 4.30. I was very glad-to,get away from that place, for there were many dead bodies lying about. Our Division were, to make an attack, and as soon as they captured,the German trench we had to follow up and connect the

trenches. When we were attacking the Ger-- mans started shelling us., and the Lewis gunners. in front of us had eight casualties out of• twelve. Just as, we were getting to our destinaft tion tk Germans started sending over Irosk• Foundries.' Soraelakting' hit the side of the trench where I' was and lifted me otf my feet and knocked alk the use out of my left foot. I shouted for a stretcher-bearer, but none came, *so I _managed to hop to the dressing station, after having knocked about for ahout three-quarters of an hour. I had lost a good deal of blOod: I was Just behind our Company- Sergennti.Mition at- the time of being hit, and a piece- of *shell' grazed the back of his hand." Private Evans; who is twenty-one years of age, enligtt-d' tware, months last November, and has been at the Front thirteen months. He had previously been employed as an engineer by Messrs. Stabs, Ltd., Scotland-road, and later by Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd. He was edu- cated at Arpley-street School, is a member of the Bewsey-road , Wesleyan Church Choir, and a teacher at the Sunday- school connected with; that Church.

PRIVATE R. LEIGH.

Eldest Of Five Soldier-Brothers Wounded.

Private Richard Leigh, of the South Lancs. Regiment, son of Mr. and Mrs. W. Leigh, 43, Porsteristreet, was wounded on August Rth, but; in a letter to his mother says he expects soon to rejoin his battalion. "It was the warmest shop I had been in since I came out," he added, "but I got my own out of the !Inns.'"

Private Leigh, who is thirty-six years of age, was wounded at Hooge on June 16th last year: lie is the eldest of five soldier brothers, the others being Charles, Robert and Samuel, in ,the South Lancs. Regiment, and William, in. the R.F.A. lie was educated at Silver-street School, and worked at the Whitecross Co.'s works, being an enthusiastio member of tbo works football team.

FARM AND GARDEN.

WHEAT IN LARGE SUPPLY.

Surplus Of Half A Million Quarters.

Although the cereal year has still lour days to run, said Monday's "Times," the returns or wheat sales by farmers and of breadstuff, arrived at the ports of the United Kingdom already aggregate a total exceeding the national requirements for the business and harvest twelvemonth. The excess is about half a million quarters, and when the seasoki completed may well be threequarters of a million, The stock g a year ago were 2,156,000 qr. —not a large total, but no serious deficiency on the 10 years' average of 2,500,000 qr.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
CROPS AND ACREAGE.

How The Country Is Adjusting Itself

To War Need.

The preliminary statement of the agricul- tural returns for England and Wales, collected in June last, shows an increase of 20,760 acres in the total area under crops .and grass. In arable land there was an increase ot 85,190 acres, largely in the place of permanent grass, which shows a decline of 64,430 acres. As waS expected after, the very large increase last year, the area under wheat shows a reduc- tion, the total area, 1,912,000 acres, being a quarter of a million acres less than in 1915. The total is, however, much greater than in recent normal years, and (except for 1915) Is the highest since 1899. Barley shows a recovery of 100,000 acres from the low record of 1915; but oats show scarcely any change. Beans and Peas show considerable reductions; the area under these two pulse crops being about seven-eighths of that returned in 1915. Potatoes and mangolds liave both fallen off by some 35,000 acres, but turnips and swedes increased slightly. Among minor crops the chief alteration is in the acre- age of mustard (whether for seed or fodder) which is more than double that of 1915. Although the area undeepermanent grass shows a decline, that portion reserved for hay shows a large increase, viz., of 175,000 acres; clovers and rotation grasses show an even greater in- crease, the total under this crop being 228,000 acres more than last year. Taking all cate- gories of clover and grass, the increase in the area for mowing, as compared with 1915,.is 400,000 acres. All classes of horses show an increase, the total being over 72,000 more than in 1915; the greatest relative change is in the young stock under one,year-of age. Cattle show arise of over 151,000, and the total number is the largest ever recorded in England and Wales. The increase is chiefly among the heifers in calf and the fattening cattle; cows in milk show a small decrease. All categories of sheep are more numerous than last year, the augmenttation being 429,000. Pigs, on the other hand, show a drop of about 10 per cent., and are practically at the level of 1913.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

THE GOOSEBERRY.

This by many is considered the best of all fruits, being found in the garden of the cot- tager, and also fit for any nobleman's table. If carefully planted all that it requires in after years is an annual mulch of rotten manure, and careful pruning, or, rather, what may be termed careful thinning out of the wood. The pruning of the gooseberry at the present day is quite different from that practised in my younger days, when every branch formed a cordon, the branches being studded with spurs from base to summit. Under that system of culture the fruits certainly were larger, and the flavour also was considerably improved. ' Nowadays, how- ever, the cry is for quantity, and to secure this the style of pruning has undergone a marked change. Now the wood in the bushes is merely thinned, all,cross and misplaced branches are out out, only as much fruit-bearing wood as is consistent with the strength of the plant being left. If extra fine fruit is looked for then thin- ning ought to be done. This is an easy matter, as the green fruits that are removed are so use- ful for cooking; in fact, in many cases, the fruits are invariably gathered green, only the best-flavoured sorts being allowed to ripen. .The advantage of thinning is all the greater in the case of those sorts of a drooping habit of growth, as, in many seasons the branches are so borne down with the weight of the fruit that the fruit becomes splashed with dirt, and en- tails a deal of labour in washing it before it can be used. Now that the fruits are swelling freely is a‘good time to apply a mulch of rotten manure, the good results of which will be noticeable in the greater size of the fruits.— Wick in "Farm and Home."

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

What a world of strife we live in! Everyone seems to be in a whirl and no one seems to know where we stand.

A motor boat was placed somewhere in the neighbourhood of the lake last Saturday.

The owners decided to leave it where it was, and gave a local painter instructions to improve its beauty.

The large crowds that journeyed up the lake- side purposely to inspect the "local cruiser" were sorely disappointed "whOn they were in- formed that public boating was prohibited.

The boating question is turning out far more serious than our worthy Council anticipated.

The general opinion is "Let all boat, or no one boat."

To whom dOes "Critic' refer when he speaks of the "public body"? This question asked a friend of mine the other day. ,

Well, who is the "public body" other than our worthy Council?

The feminine army for the cultivation of the land is increasing day by day.

The local farmers delight in competing one with another to see who can offer the best advantages

One farmer residing in High Legh sends a conveyance down for his women every morning, and drives them home again in the evening.

The harvest seems to be well under control.

To-day will be a memorable day in the history of Lymm.

Q.-31.-Sergt. T. W. Field, our local D.C.M., will be presented with his medal on the historic Cross at 2 p.m. this afternoon.

It is expected that every citizen will turn out to give him a rousing reception. ,

It has taken us a long time to do it, but remember "it's better late than never."

All that you will be asked to give will be A good cheer.

The party, headed by the South Lancashire Baud, will pfterwards proceed to "Beechwood," where a magnificent garden fete will be held to help to swell the funds of our local hospital.

I should like to see every tradesmay. in the village hoist his flag to-day in honour of our

I was pleased to see Scoutinaster Theodore Nash, who has been serving with the Scouts in France, looking so hale and hearty on his return.

A piece of shrapnel, 10-koz. in weight, has been taken out of the arm of Pte. Percy Horton, whom we reported wounded a few weeks,ago.

The relic is now in the possession of his people at Lymm.

A final warning, "Don't forget 2 o'clock to- day."

A LYMM HERO,

Quartermaster-Sergeant Field's D.C.M. : Public Presentation. At Monday's meeting of the Lymm trban Council, _ _ •

The Chairman (Councillor Lockyer) asked the reporters specially to note the arrangements which had been made in reference to the public presentation to Qr.-M.-Sergt. Field, Lancashire Fusiliers, of the Distinguished Conduct Medal, awarded to him for conspicuous bravery in Gallipoli. It appeared that a letter had been received in the first instance from Captain Cartwright, who suggested that as Colonel Alan F. McClure, Qr.-M.-Sergt. Field's late Commanding Officer, would be in the district during the week, the presentation might be made publicly by that distinguished officer. The members of the Council met Captain Cartwright on Mcinday evening at 5.30, and after consultation decided to make the presen. tation to Sergeant Field at 2 o'clock to-day (Saturday) at The Cross, in Lymm. Colonel Alan F. McClure will be asked to present the medal, and there will be a proces- sion from the Council Offices to the Market Place, assembling about 2 o'clock. A band will head the procession, in which military officers, the local Volunteers, magistrates, members of the Council, wounded soldiers, members of the Red Cross Society, and others will take part. Given good weather, the event cannot fail to be of a most interesting, pleasing and inspiring nature.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S CEREMONY.

Elaborate preparations have been in progress during, the week. The band of the South Lancashire Regiment is coming over to provide music for the garden fete at BeechwOod, and will head the procession. Invitations have been sent to officers and men of His Majesty's Forces home on leave, Volunteer. Corps, 136 y, Scouts, wounded Soldiers from Red Cross Hos- pital, magistrates, clergy, police, Guardians of the Poor, members of 'Urban District Council, Friendly Societies, and the Tradesmen's Association. The party will afterwards pro- ceed in a body to Beechwood, where a garden fete is being held for the benefit of our local Red Cross Hospital. The gallant hero is as modest as he is brave, for when out representative called at his resi- dence at Newfields and informed him what was going to take place; he absolutely declined to be made a fuss of, and certainly is not suffer- ing from a "swelled head." The story of his great deed in the Dardanelles, for which he was awarded the much coveted medal, will live for ever, for although wounded in several places, he succeeded in holding a trench for several days against terrible Turkish attacks. During this time his wounds had received no treatment. He was sent to Heaton Park, Manchester, where he waS promoted from Company-Sergeant- Major to Quartermaster-Sergeant, but his health still failed him, and 'he was at last discharged as medically unfit for further war service.

BOATING ON THE LAKE.

'Council Firmly Against Boats For Hire.

The proposed regulation in reference to boating on Lymm lake came up at Monday's meeting of the Urban Council, when Councillor Parker asked, in connection with the minutes of the :Water Committee, if anything had been done with respect to boating on the Dam. The Acting Clerk (Mr. David Mort, sur- veyor) read the resolution passed by the, General Purposes Committee on the '7th of August:—" That private rowing boats be al- towed on the lake at a charge of AZ per annum and.4xlso private motor boats at a charge of per annum, including the privilege of fishing from the boats; also that a notice board be fixed near the Dam head to that effect; no boats to be used for purposes of hire or gain and no boat to be launched without the official written consent of the Council." Councillor Parker said he would strongly object to anyone being allowed to put a boat on the lake to ply for hire. Councillor Cowper said that no boat would be allowed without ,the written consent of the Council.

Councillor Warburton hoped that propriety would be observed. If a funeral was taking place in the churchyard they did not want horns blowing or anything like, that. The following letter, signed by J. Struthers, was then read :—'With regard to your adver- tisement in the paper re motor and other boats, I have it, motor boat that I should like to put on the Dam as soon as possible, not as a private boat, but to ply for hire. Of course, the thing is all a speculation—petrol is dear, labour is dear, and the season is about over. I don't suppose I should reap any benefit for the next six months, but the thing would be advertising itself and would encourage trade in the place some way or other later on. I am prepared to give to any local charity for the use of the Dam until Christmas. We may be then able to form some plan that would benefit all partieS concerned:" - Councillor Critehley said the matter had never been advertised and he moved that the letter be left on the table. _ .

Councillor Parker said he objected to any-one having a motor boat plying. They did 'not want the place turned into a bear garden. It was a residential district and he objected to the matter being entertained for a minute. Councillor Cowper thought the minute they had passed was satisfactory. They decided that they would not have motor boats or other boats for hire on the Dam.

Councillor Groves said they made it quite clear that they did not intend the lake to be used for gain. • Councillor Warburton remarked that if this man Struthers had bought this boat on "spec." he had made a great mittake. He should first of all have ascertained what the Council had decided. The place, as Councillor 'Parker had said, was not to be turned into a bear garden. It was decided to write, stating that the proposal could not be entertained.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Why Not Cater For The Workers As

Well As The Better Classes?

OPEN LETTER TO MISTER COUNCILLOR.

By S.D.M.

Our worthy, Council have condescended to allow private boating to take place on our lake on condition that we par a fee of £3 a year for a skiff, and £5 a year for a motor boat, which, plainly speaking, means that vs:3 shall have about half a dozen boats on the lake belonging to a certain class, and you and I and the great majority of other .residents will have to be content with sitting on the banks and watching. Very nice Mister Councillor, and we like it—l don't think. No doubt you have done your best, but really it's quite a poor show. What we want is something that will reduce those big rates, and I can assure you that half a dozen boats at £3 a year won't bring in a very large amount. Why are you so "contrary" as not to allow us, the working class, and those who are responsible for your presence on the Conncil a little pleasure on our magnificent lake? Oh, yes, we like boating very much, but we cannot afford to put a boat on the lake and maintain it at your price. We can afford sixpence for an hour's joy ride. Why not try public boating? Spoil the beauty of Lymm would it, and be responsible for bringing into the village a few more visitors? Of course, we don't want. visitors. They might spend a little tpo much money and thus give our tradespeople a helping hand. Why are you always so eager to study the wants of one class and not the other? Surely you don't surmise that they 'are the main support of Lymm? As a residential village Lymm is just about done, and it is about time that you changed your working methods, and started some others that will prove a little bit more profitable. Look at Beechwood and lots of your other large, residences. What good have they been to the village I should like to ask ?—untenanted and everlastingly empty, which means that there is nothing coming in. The time will surely come, Mister Councillor, when the working classes will see that they are not being treated fairly, and then. . . . It will be proved in Lymm as it is being proved everywhere that the working man still has a finger in the pie. You meet together every month, with a committee meeting almost every week, and you talk things over, and be- have yourselves like gentlemen and good citizens (sometimes), but we never seem to get any farther, nor do those huge rates get any smaller. You have played on the old fiddle too long, in the hope of getting a good tune, but it's hope- less, so I advise you to get a new string put in, and be very careful not to break it. Per- haps if we wait long enough you will come to see the wisdom of using a portion of our lake for an open-air swimming bath for the rising generation. But perhaps that would be an un- necessary luxury, and greatly inconvenience a section of our residents when they take their usual walk. Yes, these obstacles will come if you let them. Keep on allowing them to over- rule you and see where they will land you. What about these lads who come out of the munition works every evening covered from head to foot with oil? Where are they to have their bath? You know, as well as I know, that

join, or any other information be received con- cerning him, such information will be at once communicated to you." Prior to enlisting he was employed as a carter by Mr. Dalton, con- fectioner, of Eagle Brow. He enlisted in the Rifle Brigade in July, 1915, but was sub- sequently transferred to another regiment, and was drafted out to France on the .19th February, 1916. He would have celebrated his twenty-first birthday next Sunday. His brother, Corporal Wm. Jackson, of the Cheshire Regiment, has been wounded once, and returns to the Front again this week-end. His uncle, Private Walter Jackson (Cheshire Regiment) was killed in action some time ago, and his cousin, Private Wm. Read (Cheshire Regiment) we report wounded in to-day's issue.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE PERCY CLARE.

Leaves For France After Farewell Party.

Impressive scenes were witnessed on the Station platform on Thursday morning, when Private •Percy Clare, of the Cheshire Regiment, one of our heroes of the push who has been home on a month's furlough, left Lymm to rejoin his unit somewhere in France. Quite a large crowd had assembled on the platform, and as the gallant soldier arrived, accompanied by his immediate relatives, he was greeted from all sides by wishes of luck, Gocispeed and safe return. He was in full marching order, and carried his rifle and kit bag, and certainly looked a typical English "Tommy." He wore a smile throughout the whole trying ordeal, and as the train steamed out of the Station he stood at the window and waved his hand to the croivd until he was almost out of sight. Farewell Party,

On Wednesday evening large party of friends and past and present comrades assembled in the large tea room belonging to Mr. David Daniels, confectioner, Booth's Hill, where a farewell party was held in his honour. The room was tastefully , decorated with a large amount of bunting of national colours, and pro- jecting with considerable prorainency right across one wall, worked in colours of red, white and blue was the following inscription, "Long live the King." Past and present com- rades attending were ex-Sergeant Rickard Percival (time expired), ex-Private Richard Shaw (time expired), ex-Private James Taylor (wounded and discharged medically unfit), ex- Private S. D. Moston (discharged medically unfit), Private James Gilberts (home from the Front), and Private Alex. Jones (home on fur- lough). Songs and recitations were rendered by Miss ,Kitty Jolley (her singing of "Bonny Mary -of Argyle" being a special feature), Miss M. A. Appleton, Miss Emily Leather. Mr. Ben Jolley, Mr. A. Pilkington and ex-Private James Taylor, and the humour of the evening was provided entirely by Private Alex Jones, wriln created endless laughter with "My ex- periences in training." The remainder of the evening was devoted to dancing, which was kept up until the early hours of the morning. At the conclusion Private Percy Clare thanked Mr. and Mrs. Daniels for their kindness and the guests for their presence, and remarked that he had had a very good time whilst he had been home, and would return to duty again in the morning ,(apparently forgetting that it was then morning), with a good heart and pleasant memories. Mr. Ben Jolley then thanked him and all concerned for their •Oleasant evening's entertaiftnient, and in hand- ing him a present which had been subscribed for amongst his pals, wished him on behalf ( f the boys God speed and a safe return. The party broke up to the singing of "Should auld acquaintance be forgot?"

===== 0002601_19160902_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
A . COUNCILLOR'S PROTEST

Against Single Men In Munition Works.

At the monthly meeting of the Lymm Urban Council, on Monday, Councillor Cowper reminded the members that they had given him the post of a Visitor of National Registration. He had visited many cottages and was very surprised to find such a number of young fellows employed in munition shops, while married men, with three or four children, were going out to the Front. These young fellows ought to be combed out. (Hear, hear.) Either women or discharged soldiers could do the work these young men were doing. It was a time when every Eng- lishman, Irishman, or Scotsman, and every man from the Colonies, should come forward. If our full strength was used now the war would be over before the year was out. (Ap- plause.) The Council endorsed Councillor Cowper's view.

HEATLEY WORKMAN REFUSED

CERTIFICATE.

...-...-rbert'Heaton, in the employ of a firm at 'Healey, complained to the Warrington Muni- tions Court, on Monday, :that his certificate of discharge had been unreasonably withheld. He stated that the company refused to give him "back-time" money, amounting to 13s. His average wages were £1 3s. 11d, per week, with a war bonus. • He did not know whether he was paid the standard rate of, wages which ruled in the district. His object in wishing to leave was to enable him to get a little more money than he was now earning. A representative of the company said com- plainant was a blacksmith's striker and his wages, with overtime, from. June Ist to August 2nd, was 30s, 2d. The man was away from August 2nd to August 14th without assigning any reason for his absence. He gave notice to leave on the last-named day, but the Engineer said he could not accept it, as he had no one to put in his place, as some night- work had to be clone. The certificate was refused.

The Late Mr. A. W. Callender.--,Before open- ing the business of the Lymm Urban Council on Monday evening, the Chairman (Councillor R Lockyer) said it was his sad duty to refer to the death of one of their most respected residents, Mr. Callender, who for many years took a peat interest in the welfare of the district. 111 r. Callender was a most popular gentleman and would be missed by all who knew him. He (the chairman) moved that the Clerk should convey the sincere sympathy of the Connell to Mrs. Callender and her family. The resolution was passed in the usual manner. The Chairman added that he wished to thank the members of the Council and staff and the Fire Brigade for showing their respect by attending the funeral.

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

Mr. G. Pilling (chairman) presided at the usual meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday, other members present being Messrs. J. H. Clarke, F. Pearson, C. H. Hallows, J. McDermid, F. Roserand• H. J. WeStbrook. The Clerk (Mr. Hough) stated thit he had again written to the Town Clerk of Warring,toit with reference to thelreatment of house refuse and the latter replied that owing to the-diffi- culties raised by a Government department the Corporation would be unable to treat the refuse coming in from Stockton Heath in the same quantity as heretofore. They granted another fourteen days from 16th August. Mr. Pearson understood that the Corporation would be willing to deal with a small quantity every week—abolit six loads—and he proposed that they be asked to do so. This suggestion was adopted. The sub-committee appointed to endeavour to get a suitable site as ,a tip for the disposal of house refuse reported thttt they had been 'un- able to do so up to the present. _

With the exception Of passing a couple of payments, there was no other business before the Committee.

STOCKTON HEATH STUDENT'S SUCCESS.

Mr. Fred Albinson, of 36, Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath, has received the following letter from Mr. C. Richard Lewis, Vice-Prin- cipal of the Municipal Technical School, Wid- nes :—" Dear Albinson,—l have great pleasure in informing you that you have succeeded in taking the Bronze Medal in Grade I. Alkali Manufacture awarded by the City and Guilds of London Institute. They announce it as third prize as last year, and, so far as I can gather from the list, the first and second prizes are again withheld. I presume in any case, how- ever, they would have been attached to the Final Grade. Allow me to offer you hearty congratulations on the part of the school and the Higher Education Sub-Committee, who will be very pleased to know once more that we have students of such calibre in the Evening Technical School." Another Warrington student, named Allison, carried off the medal last year. Mr. Fred Albinson, who is employed at the United Alkali Company's Works, Wid- nes, has attended the technical classes in that town for some time past, and his many friends in Stockton Heath and Warrington will'be very pleased to hear of his success. This,examina- tion is held at various centres throughout the country and the final selection is made in London; so that it is a special distinction to gain the medal. Mr. Albinson worked for three years under Mr. Frederick Greville Ruddock, F.I.C. public analyst and official agricultural analyst, Warrington.

GRAPPENHALL.

AMERICAN TEA.

2,89 Raised In The Rectory Garden.

As the result of the efforts of a number of enterprising Grappenhall ladies, at the head of whom was Mrs. Pigot, an American tea was held in the Grappenhall Rectcry Gardens on Thursday, in aid of the British Red Cross Society and St. John Ambulance Association. These two societies make an annual appeal on, what is called "Our Day," and the Grappen- hall district—of which Mrs. Pigia is the town- ship leader—will shortly hand over £S9 to those two deserving societies as the result of Thui s- day's effort. The weather was fine, and people visited the Rectory in goodly numbers. Various stalls had been erected in the grounds, these includ- ing a refreshment stall, cake stall, sweet stall, farming stall and fancy, wcirk stall. Teas were served on the lawn, following which various games were played, such as croquet, clock-golf, quoits, &c. The fete lasted from three to seven o'clock.

PADGATE.

Illness of Private Young.—Mrs. young, Station-road, Padgate, has just had a letter from her son, Private F. Young, who is ill hospital in India, suffering from malarial fever. We are pleased, to report that he is im- proving satisfactorily.

Wesleyan Sisterhood Social.—The opening social for the winter session was held on Tues- day afternoon, the tea being very kindly given by Mrs. J. Hamblett, of Padgate. The meeting was presided over by the President (Mrs. Plinston). Songs were rendered, by Mrs. Greet" Mrs. Thomason and Mrs. lialeldene. Mrs. Vi• J. Bate acted as accompanist. There were 3 good number present and the meeting through- out was very enjoyable.

War Relief Society.—The monthly whist drive organised by this society to raise funds to help on the good work which is being done, was held in the Church House on ThursdaY evening. The winners were :—Ladies: Miss B. Grounds and Miss Carey. Gentlemen: Mr. J• Webb and Mrs. J. Webb. Mr. J. Webb acted as M.C. The prizes, which were given, were presented by Mrs. HaddOck. The net profits amounted to nearly 30s.

Visit From Latchford Wesleyans.—The meta- bers of the Latchford Wesleyan Senior S.S. Class paid a visit to the Senior and Society Classes of the Padgate Wesleyan School, and were entertained to tea in the schoolroom, the tea being very kindly given by Miss Bennett, of Bruche Heath. After tea there were music, games, competitions, &e. Miss Nicholson sang "Annie Laurie" and Miss B. London sang "Come, sing to me." The Rev. A. J. Morton created roars of laughter by his rendering of 'Father O'Flynn," and Miss M. Appleton Was very successful in Home, sweet home." MiSS' E. Holt recited "In the children's hospital." The accompanist for the evening was Miss Nicholson. Refreshments were provided dur- ing the interval, and the evening was tSOroughlY enjoyed.

Boy Scouts.—A very interesting and instruc- tive programme for the 2nd Padgate Troop Of Boy Scouts was drawn up, for last Saturdal afternoon. The object was to ascertain the whereabouts of two visiting cards, which had been hidden at different points. The patrol. leaders were given a brief description of the objects of interest on the routes to be followed; each route being divided* into three parts. itt the end of the third' part of the route plans were hidden at a given point, and' each hel was then given a plan showing trees, gates and telegraph pores for 101 Y yards from- this point. They were then tad to work on their own initiative. In spite of -the inclement weather the boys entered heartily Into the search, and though all were more or Tess soaked by the rain they trudged on from point to point until the goal was reached, the time spent being about 31 hours. The prizes, very kindly given bi Mr. F. Blackwell, of School House, Padgate, were won by Patrol Leader C. Dakin and Scou D. Murray and L. Golbourn.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS.

Colonel Arthur Stanleigh and Judge Lamar Stanleigh, cousins, of Virginia, are bitter enemies ever the posseesion of The Diamond from the Sky, all a•ssive jewel heirloom found centuries ago in a 'alien meteor. Colonel Stanleigh, when a daughter is born, buys the male heir of a gipsy king, that 4,118 may keep the jewel. He plans to rear Esther, re girl, in secrecy. Esther and The Diamond 'rein the Sky are stolen by Hagar, the gipsy rnother. Years later Hagar returns Esther and the diamond, but of the old group only Dr. Lee is Blair Stanleigh kills Dr. Lee and steals the jewel. Then Blair Stanleigh and Arthur Stan- leigh, the false heir, fight a duel. Arthur, thinking he has killed his cousin, flees in a racing motor car. In his mad flight the machine goes over a river bank, and that night the moon shines on a body floating down the stream. Arthut is found by Esther and Hagar and nursed to health. Hagar tells him his real identity. Arthur swears to uake himself worthy of Esther, and leaves, taking the diamond, and meets Blair Stanleigh. Blair takes him to a great ball, where they meet Vi• ian Marston, a New York belle, wearing the diamond. 4agar is there, too, telling fortunes.. Vivian has just seated herself when' a brawny firearm clutches The Diamond from the Sky. A greater confusion than that caused by the loss of the diamond arises- when the Sheriff of Fairfax arrives at the ball and demands the arrest of Arthur Stanleigh. He escapes, partly through the efforts of Hagar, and later finds work and refuge as a farm hand. Luke Lovell drops the diamond into a mail-bag. The gem clings by its clasp to a rent in the bag, and ja afterwards found by Qua,bba, the hunchback organ-grinder. He does not retain it long. Clar- ence, his monkey, drops it in an owl's nest. Arthur, working on a farm as "John Powell,'; masks him- self and goes to the Fairfax tournament, where he wins the highest honour. He is betrayed by' Blair Stanleigh, and amid great confusion the young Virginians fight to bar Arthur's escape without avail. Hagar, holding proofs of Blair Stanleigh's guilt, is brutally attacked by the young man and her mind destroyed. The diamond finds its way to a Chinese opium den, whence Blair steals ,it. Vivian marries him, and they escape from Tom Blake in a train which robbers board and take de diamond, with Other jewels and money. Pursued by a Sheriff's posse, the train robbers lose the diamond and treasure, the latter being found by " John Powell." Blair presses his attentions upon Esther, who spurns his professed love. Arthur Purchases Stanleigh Hall. Luke Lovell purchases. the diamond, only to lose it through the treachery of a partner. Quabba is knocked down by a tram- car, and by a false message Blair induces ;Esther to visit him. The girl is attacked and then rescued by Tom Blake.

Inquiries at the offices of the Fair Hope Oil Syn- dicate by Quabba,lair and Marmadukti all led to the same result. "John Powell" had gone to the mines. For different reasons they all wanted to meet the rich young man, and Esther. Blair and Marmaduke all left for the mining country. Quabba should have remained :behind, but_seeing Blair and Vivian driving together, he scented danger, and followed by another train, and at an alighting sta- tion found he had to trudge thirty miles in the absence of a conveyance. Making light of the task, but fearing for "the young mistress," he started on his long tramp. Blair set out in a motor car for the Mines, but before starting had an interview with Vivian. "No matter what I do to gain the money or the diamo-.i you must not" interfere. On my part, I Will intere.! myself in his affairs either with your aid or with out. We will work together, and I will promise you that in the end, if all goes well, we will have our desires. Mine is to have money, to be Lady Rtan- leigh, and above all to possess The Diamond irom the Sky. Your desire, you say, is to possess m:1. There is one way, help me to get the diarnoad; help me to get John Powell in my power —we nl.tv need his money for all our purposes!" To Esther the short journey seemed long. Wbat would be the result of her meeting with Arthur? Lonely, apprehensive, even disheartened, she folt that to be coolly received by Arthur wouli be ti.e laiit straw. The secret joy she had felt wine, ,realising that she was the rightful heir of Stan'eio. Hall had passed. She had been dewed r right so long that she could feel no gl,t,•iness it the thought of being accepted as Esther Stankigh rather than Esther Harding, the poor gipsy girl. Only Arthur, only the love she bore for him, made her steadfast. She felt she could' go away and die of a broken heart if he had changed ,and with his millions had groan arrogant, selfish, and cold to- wards her. She regretted, she had gone into the wilderness to seek him.' Might she not have better stayed in Los Angeles till Arthur returned, and meet him there? At the station nearest the mines Esther and Marmaduke, strangers . one to the other, left the train and boarded the stage-coach. Near the blacksmith's shop Blair's car broke down, and going to the shop to seek assistance was astonished to come face to face with Luke Lovell.

"You here! What are you doing?" "Hush, I am working with the blacksmith," re- plied Luke.

"Hiding probably," thought Blair, but he did not voice the thought. Just then the blacksmith's little girl called her father home to his sick wife, and Blair espied a coach in difficulties. A linch- pin had broken and the driver called for a new pin. Luke started to adjust a new pin. Blair Stanleigh. within the darkened shop, for he half expected recognition by someone, saw Esther, as she shrank back 'from the window at sight of Luke. Luke in turn was busy at his work at the wheel, and gave no attention to the passengers, except one, an Eng- lishman with side whiskers, who hung out of the coach and annoyed him with foolish questions. When Luke came into the shop for tools Blair drew him on one side and whispered: "There is someone in that coach who will ruin our game with Arthur Stanleigh. You know what to do?" _ _

Luke had a dim- remembrance of the English lawyer, and not having seen Esther in the coach, deemed Blair referred to that individual. Luke nodded grimly, replaced the broken linch-pin, and called to the driver, "It's all right," grandly wav- ing aside the driver's offer of a tip, for Blair had paid for the work with a twenty-dollar bill. Down by the coast Quabba, pausing in his jour- ney to the mines, was fishing. The sport was good save that Quabba was annoyed by the aggressive boldness or friendliness, Quabba could hardly tell which, of a persistent pelican. It was a great fat white pelican, which reminded Quabba very much of a certain pompous, long-nosed, corpulent southern magistrate, who wore a white waistcoat, and fined Quabba for playing hand-organ music without a licence. Quabba remembered this magis- trate with extreme distaste, and the pelican resem- bled him so much that Quabba hated the bird all the more in consequence. The pelican was a wise bird; he waited till Quabba should land a fish and Patience was rewarded. A great quivering fish came out of the water at the end of the line. Caught ,in its gills and partly hanging from its mouth was a chain of antique workmanship. It was the Diamond from the Sky. Quabba, grasped at his catch, but before he could. move the pelican had swooped down and bore away fish and jewel. Along the crest of- the mountain, away-from Luke Lovell and Blair, the stage-coach rolled along the dizzy height. Beside a precipitoua declivity the broken linch-pin did its work. The wheel came off and spun away, the coach toppled ovei, the tongue snapped off short, and the great swaying vehicle fell over and rolled, gathering momentum as it rolled, while Esther and the English lawyer, hud- dled in its groaning interior, spun round and round with the rolling coach too terrified to shriek. Then the coach struck a great boulder and smashed as an egg might. A spurt of dust and then stillness. ,

FOR LOVE, AND MONEY.

It was a -badly bruised and bleeding Blair who came to his senses on the hall floor of the Old mansion turned into a lodging-house. As he stag- gered to his feet, dust was still falling and the shaking timbers of the rotten old staircase and landing threatened again to bury him. The dazed Young man had sufficient knowledge of city life to know that within a few minutes the police would be on the scene. He had no intention of being arrested for complicity in a brawl, and he was alarmed lest he had been recognised. None of, his assailants were to be seen; but he could heat the shrill voice of the landlady shouting to the neigh- bours. Before he had climbed clear of the wreck- age the officers were at the door, and while the hYsterical landlady held the barred door against the battering police, Blair left the house, and, climbing over a series of fences, gained a back street.

Standing at the corner was a throbbing motor car, with the driver screwing down a valve-head. Bowling over the astonished chauffeur, he leapt into the car and drove away. One idea was in his mind: to reach Mrs. Randolph before Esther, and bluff through the accusation she would make had he been recognised. At breakneck speed he drove along the roads, had drawn up in front of the house, and donned dust-coat and cap before the car containing Esther and Quabba arrived. He at once saw that he had been recognised. "How dare you attack me in that house?" de- Manded the girl. . . .

"I do not understand," came the lying retort. "You do. You tied Quabba up and attacked Inc." .

"Who? Me? Why, I've been out all the after- noon in my new car," insisted Blair. "The poor man has a bad memory," said Quabba. "That may be, but Mrs. Randolph milk, be told Of it," said Esther, leading the ,way into the house, followed by the organ-grinder and the pro- testing Blair. Mrs. Randolph assured Esthei. that she must be mistaken. Blair was not that kind of 'young man. "No, indeed," said the villain, "it is the last thing I should think of doing to the girl I hope to marry." "Marry you, you beast! I would kill you first and myself after." There warP no mistaking Esther's attitude towards the young man who had exbressed the desire to-marry her, and she left him alone with Quabba, who gave Blair a parting shot. 'My eyes did not deceive me. - I owe you two for Your brutality, and onecday I'll be square. You liar !"

Blair chuckled as, lie- remembered Mrs. Ran- dolph's indiDaant defence of him. "Surely. Esther, you and this uncouth eipsy person, with his horrid monkey, must be mistaken. No Stan- leigh would do such a thing. I am sure." Two days later Quabba, with part of the money so mysteriously sent Esther, left Richmond for Los Angeles, and'at the end of the week, despite Mrs. Randolph's objections, the girl followed west in search of Arthur. About the same time Blair decided to act upon Vivian's suggestion, and that vivacious young person received a telegram in. Inpeoree(reerrececeeceoee
