ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKING MEN'S ASSOCIATION.

N.A. CENTRE.

Although the elements were anything but favour- able last week-end, reports from most places were good, both in canals and rivers. Match fishing is taking up most of the week-ends at present, and members are very glad of a change, so that they can again visit their favourable haunts. Several speci- mens have reached the , set-board during the last few weeks, and this makes members eager to get back again to ordinary angling, in place of the hurry and bustle of contest fishing.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEE.

Not many locals have fished this river lately, but we heard of one angler coming away last week with a basket full of fair, sizable fish.

THE GOWY.

Several members who stayed the week-end here had poor sport, with the exception of one, who managed to bag eight fair roach.

SHROPSHIRE UNION.

This canal is fishing very well at Llangollen and Chirk; roach from Boz. to 11b. 3oz. have ben taken.

NORTON.

From Saturday last up to time of writing this canal has never fished better, members taking up to twenty roach, ranging from 3oz. to 7oz.

THE WEAVER.

The riverNbere is in fine trim, one member weigh. ing a perch well over 11b. We have a report to hand of a match on Sunday last, when the winner had 41b. 14oz.

PLANTS FOR FRANCE.

The Board of Agriculture and Fisheries have been informed by the Foreign Office that the French Government have kindly consented to authorise until further notice the entry into France of all plants forwarded from England to British hospitals and cemeteries in France, although not accompanied by .the certificates _ _ _

required under the- Phylloxera Convention. When consignments of _ plants are being sent to France for other purposes, consignors should be careful to make the necessary arrangements to procure the required certificates.

FOOTBALL.

Rugby And Association Teams ANA.a.

To-day

===== 0002601_19160902_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
AT LEIGH AND TRANNIERE.

The football season opens to-day (Saturday), and Warrington Northern Union team is to visit Leigh. The train leaves Bank Quay at 1.15, and it is hoped that a large number of enthusiasts will jour- ney with the team, which is as follows:

Full-back, - B. Jolley; threequarter-backs, Taberner, Higham, Tranter and Baker; half-backs, Young and Callon; forwards, °Harmer, Cunliffe, Green, Matthews, Clegg and Dagnall.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSOCIATION.

Warrington Town also have their first fixture away, Tranmere Rovers being their opponents. The men from whom the players will be selected are:— Kite, Leah, Millward, Ashworth, G. Whitfield. Ditch- field, Eccleston, Pte. Kendrick, Aspinall, Billy Sid- dall (Eccles Borough), Owen, Gregson and Keenan (Blackpool and Warrington). It will be noticed that four useful players of last season are missing from the list—Cookson, Foster, T. Whitfield and Broster—these having joined the Army during the summer. The acquisition of Siddall,,is a good step, for he did valuable work last year for Eccles Borough, a team which proved one of the most forinidable against the Town. Next week the Town will be al home to Tranmere Rovers. The ground will still be in Hood-lane, but slightly farther afield, just behind the Rose Inn. As the home matches of the Rugby and Association teams clash, an effort is to be made to change the fixtures of the latter, so as to permit of their being at home when the Rugby team is away, and so on.

KNIOR FOOTBALL.

Warrington And District League Teams.

A general meeting of the above was held on ,Monday night it the Peter-street Dining Rooms, when there waS a good attendance' of club dele- gates. The following clubs have signified their intention to join the League:.—St. Elphin's, Howley United, Penketh Juniors, Bewsey Adult School, Latchtord Cadets, Longford Juniors, Mersey Juniors, Fairfield Juniors, and Runcorn Tannery. The following gentlemen were elected as officers for the season ;—Chaiiman, Mr. R. Simpson; vice- chairman, Mr. Ford, Runcorn; treasurer, Mr. S. C. Essenhigh; assistant secretary, Mr. C. Hough; secretary, Mr. J. Smith, 7, Chapel-lane, Stockton Heath, Warrington. A number of rules were drawn up and approved of, and fixtures.for September were arranged. The meeting was then adjourned until Monday, Septem- ber 11th, when rules and fixtures will be completed, and further applications for membership will be considered. All the delegates were very enthusi- astic for the welfare of the League, and a very strenuous season is expected.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
WARR I NGTON WEATHER.

Why August Made Us Unhappy

And Wet.

Whether it's cold or whether it's hot, we'll have to weather it whether or not. Philosophy is the best cure after all, and if .people were upset by the very unpleasant weather which prevailed in August, they have now the satis- faction of knowing that the month is over and that not very much harm has been done. Until expert. opinion is procured one is apt to imagine that August was abnormal in its rain- fall. That was not the case in Warrington, especially in view of the statement that in Lon- don it was the worst experience they have had for forty years. The whole rainfall for the month was noticeably lower than during the same period last year. It was not a particular downfall, but the showers between that iriteN fered with harvesting operations. To . Mr. Albert E. Hands, at. the ,Fever Hospital, whose duties include that of recording the weather conditions of' Warrington, we are indebted for some interesting details. Having rained rather frequently up to the 17th July, we had practically dry weather until the -12th. August, 'inclusive, the, gauge register- ing about half an inch of rain during that period. From the 'l2th. August to 20th August,' nine days,: it rained 'every day—the 21st and 22nd were fine, and thefl the rain returned from the 23rd. .to the 28th, inclusive—six days.' August 29th, 30th and 31st were fine and, there- fore we find that the total rainfall for August was 2.52 inches.

Now a comparison with last year is of in- terest. In August, 1915, we had a rainfall of 3.3 inches, and it rained on 22 separate days, as against 15 last month. But the effect was different on the present occasion. because although we had quite a normal rainfall, the rain came in two solid periods of nine and six days respectively—the former being just at the opening of the harvest season. This fact made the occurrence particularly noticeable. In August, 1914, rain fell on 15 days, registering 2.68 inches.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
Prevailing Winds.

It is interesting further to note the direction of the winds during the 'mit month. In the fine period—the first eleven days—the previtiling winds were S.E. Rain fell on the 12th, and the wind changed to S.S.E. Then it went round to S.S.W., and continued in a westerly direction during the whole of the rainy period. On the 21st it went to N.W., and we had a fine day. On the 22nd it veered round to S.E.—that being anotherfine day. Then we struck another rainy period of six days, when it remained practically the whole time from the S.S.W. On. the 29th the wind veered °nee- more to N.-E., and fine weather returned. Does Gun Fire Cause Rain?

It is suggested that the above facts dispose satisfactorily of the theory that Unsettled weather is due in a metvure to the heavy gun fire in France, Belgium and the North Sea., It was only during the fine spells of weather that the wind was blowing direct from the battle- fields. The winds escorting rain came from westerly points—and probably the Atlantic Ocean supplies the moisture.

Doctors Orders. An /old coloured • man was .found by the preacher prowling in his barnyard late one night.

Uncle Calhoun," said the preacher sternly, Tit can't be good for your rheumatism to be prowling round here in the rain and cold:" "Doctor's orders, sah," the old man answered. " Doctor's orders?" said the preacher. "Did he tell you to go prowling round all night?" ' "No, sah, not exactly, sah," said Uncle Cal.; "but he done ordered me chicken broth."

Acting-Sergeant Armstrong stated that at six o'clock'• on Tuesday I morning of last week he cycled along the Liverpool-road and saw de- fendant on the canal side near Sankey Bridges. He asked him where he had been all night and he replied,j "I have been sleeping out here." Witness.searched him and found the gold chain in his trouser pocket. He asked him where he gat it, and he said that he had it for some time. Witness said that a shop had been broken .into in'Lovely-lane, and defendant said he 'knew' nothing 'about it. ' The Prisoner (violently): You're a liar! Witness, Continuing, said that when he brought prisoner to the Police Station, and the charge was preferred he said "Yes, that's right; 1 did it."

Constable Willis stated that at about 4.4 d a.m. on Tuesday, 22nd August, he pas.sed the shop of Messrs. Stubbs in Lovely-lane. N. Everything was then in order. Passing again some few minutes later he saw that the plate-glass win- dow in the front had been slashed. There was a hole sufficiently large td put one's hand into. On looking at the goods in the window he saw that something had been removed. Witness searched the neighbourhood and sent for assistance. While this witness was giving eviden.le accused was smiling and apparently enjoyrng the proceedings. The Chairman (Mr. H. Roberts) rebuked him for his levity and said it seemed that he did not quite appreciate the gravity of his position. Accused did not reply to the Clerk when asked if he had anything to say, and he was then committed for trial.

IN THE COURTS.

WARNING TO FIRMS.

Military And Employment Of

Non-Exempted Men.

Firms which continue to employ non- exempted men would do well to take heed of the warning which Captain McPhail gave at the Borough Police Court on Wedn6Sday, when Wilfred Barrow, 25,- Catherine-street, was fined 40s. and handed over to the military authorities for being an' absentee. Detective-Sergeant Reddington having stated

that he arrested Barrow at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iran Co., Ltd., Captain McPhail said the military were having a lot of trouble thro-s.:gh firms in the town employing. men without exemption certificates, and unless the matter ceased they would proceed against some of them. Apparently it-was the only way of weeding out the men liable for service. In the case of Barrow, his badge, which- he had when working for a Patricroft firm, expired two months ago, and he now had no certificate. Of course, the firm 'should never have employed him. All employers had been warned several times. in the town, and such employment must cease immediately.

HOLE IN SHOP WINDOW.

Xlieged Thelt.Of Gold Chain From

Pawnbroker's.

Charged on remand before the magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday with shop breaking and larceny, Arthur Williams, 29, nco'gettled abode, was returned for trial at the next 'Quarter 'Sessions. Mr. Duncan McLachlan, manager for Messrs. Stubbs, Bros., pawnbrokers and jewellers, 18, Lovely-lane, in reply, to Chief Constable Nicholls, deposed that he lives at Liverpool and saw the shop closed as usual on the night,of Aisgust 21st, everything being secure. When he came in the next morning the shop window had been broken and a gold chain valued at .25 18s. 6d., which had been 'ranging from a small rail in the window, was missing. The hole in the plate-glass window was about nine inches by three. The chain, now produced by the police, was the one which had been exposed for sale in the window.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR HIS HEALTH.

hy Irishman Came To Warrington And Why He Left.

An Irishman named Michael Hunt, 53, Nicholson-street, was charged at the Borough Police , Court on Thursday with being an absentee under the Military Service Act and with failing to produce a certificate of regts-. tration.

Detective-Sergeant Reddington stated that in consequence of receiving a list of names sent from Messrs. Pearson and Knowle of men who were employed there and not badged by the firm, he made inquiries and interviewed the prisoner. He saw him at the works on Tuesday. Defendant said that he had been about seven weeks in this country and he had no papers to show why he should not be in the Army. He had a certificate from a doctor in Ireland, and said he came to this country for the benefit of, his health. Witness brought him, to Captain McPhail, and he consented to be examined by -the Medical Board. He, was passed for general service. When asked to produce a certificate of registration he said he di4 not know he should , have one. The doctor's certificate was dated Bth lAugust, aid he- had sent over to Ireland for it.

Chief COnstable Nicholls said the onus -rested upon the prisoner to show that he was not ordinarily resident in this country, or why he should not be made liable for military service. He said that he had come over for the benefit of his health and for change of air, but he went to Pearson and Knowles to do a hard job. They did not want to take .any unfair advantage of him. The Chief Constable added that they hai asked for instructions from the Home Office in another case and the Home Office wired that it was for the magistrates to decide whether the man was ordinarily resident in this country. They were having to send their own boys away, who were getting a shilling a day, _while other men were getting £2 a weeks They took the view that this man was liable for service.

Defendant said he could get evidence from the Chairman of his County Council and two doctors, from Ireland, and, in reply to the Clerk of the Court, said he did not know any- one here who could substantiate the statement that he only came to reside here temporarily. The Chief Constable said defendant told the recruiting, authorities that he intended to stay: Referring to the case already mentioned, Mr. Nicholls said that the Secretary of State took the view that when the man had come here three months prAvious for the purpose of work- ing he became ordinarily resident in Great Britain. -

' The Court held that the case was proved, and ordered defendant to be handed over to the military authorities. They made an allowance for his not knowing the technicalities as to having a certificate and, therefore, did not im- pose any fine.,

===== 0002601_19160902_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOP WINDOW SMASHED.

Disappointed Lodger's Revenge And

Its Result. •

At the 'Borough Police Court on Thursday, a middle-aged man named Ernest Applin Green, no settled abode, and described as a ship's steward„ was charged with -behaving in a dis- orderly manner in Friars' Gate the previous evening, and with wilfully breaking a plate- glass window in the premises. 17, Bnrbauld- street.

Special Constable Dendy said the prisoner

===== 0002601_19160902_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
was disorderly in Friars' Gate about 9.40 p.m. He was shouting and throwing up his arms, being so violent that witness took him into custody. He was informed that accused had broken a plate-glass window, and the charge of wilful damage was also preferred. The win- dow was valued at £3. _

Defendant said that he had gone to this place and asked for lodgings and paid his money. When he got to the lodging-house the people there started joking with him. He asked the woman, Mrs. McCabe, to give him his number as he wished to go to bed. She told him to go back and ask for Billy. He was made a laugh- ing stock, and when he asked for his money back she threvi him twopence. He lost his temper. Defendant added that he went through the South African campaign and had been two months in this last affair. His health broke down and he had also lost two sons. Mrs. McCabe called him a tferman. _

Mrs. McCabe said that when defendant paid her for the bed she wanted him to sign where he had been the previous night. He told her to put down Liverpool. He wanted to go to bed and she said he would have to wait' his turn. He then asked for his money, and when she went to get it she saw him with about a shill- ing's worth of puddings in his hand. She told him to put them down and prisoner then took up a chair and broke the window. She denied that she called him a German. Prisoner was sentenced to one month's im- prisonment, with hard labour.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPRISONMENT FOR FAILING TO REGISTER.

As a warning to those who failed to register, the magistrates at Monday's Borough Police Court sentenced John Whittaker (49), no fixed residence, to fourteen days' imprisonment. De- fendant was further charged by Acting-Sergeant Clark with begging in the public thoroughfare and using abusive language. On this charge lw was sentenced to one month's imprisonment— making six weeks in all. •

John Bent de Lascy, 30, Norris-street, was summoned at the Borough Police Court on Thursday for failing to notify the authorities of change of address, as required by the Regis- tration Act. "IT'S A LIE "-AND HE AX ON.

William Mason, residing at Grappenhall, was charged with having, at Latchford, on the 19th August, absented himself without leave from his work. When asked if he was guilty he replied "It's a lie." A representative of the firm stated that the

defendant gave ,the usual seven days' notice and worked the 'week out. He gave no reason for wishing to leave and Witness did not accept the notice.

The President (Mr. T. J. Ridgway): Why didn't you accept the notice? The Representative: It would have been stultifying the Munitions Act to allow a,man to leave work without giving a satisfactory reason for wishing to do so. The case was dismissed.

Married Man To Join His Six

Brothers.

Mr. F. A. Frost presided at the Tribunal sitting in the Town Hall on Wednesday, other members present being Messrs. J. J. Whitley, J. Elwell and W. Bradbuyy. ;Pork Butcher's Appeal Refused.

He submitted the Tribunal could not entertain the present appeal by the employer. The (Tribunal decided that they could not hear the employer's application. He had had an opportunity of appealing to. Liverpool, but failed to do so. On the domestic grounds the man was granted a further month exemption —this to be final. Six Brothers in thc Army.

A married man, 26, appealed on domestic grounds, and informed the Tribunal that he had six brothers in the Army. One of these had been killed arid two were wounded and in hospital. THE CHESHIRE SIDE.

Latchford C.O. Passed For Combatant Service.

At the Runcorn Rural Tribunal, at the Work- house, Dutton, on Thursday, a Latchford man, labourer, appealed on conscientious grounds. He stated that he could not under any circum- stances do anything that was contrary to the teaching of Jesus Christ, who had taught us to love our enemies. He was an experienced iron worker and furnace man, and was willing to continue in his present employment as long as he was in a civil capecity. He attended the services of the International Bible Students' Association. Explaining why he ldrft the Sal- vation Army, the objector remarked "They don't believe in eternal torment where I go to, but they do in the Salvation Army." The Clerk: You are working in a controlled works.—The Objector: I don't handle anything concerning the war. The objector was passed for combatant ser- vice in the Army.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
CAIRO-STREET CHAPEL.

Rev. Irvine Lister On What It Stands For.

In a letter to the members of his congrega- tion on entering upon the third year of his ministry, the Rev. Irvine Lister says : " Our Church is a Free Church, and it is also a Christian Church, and its litragical services are, with the common consent of all who hear them, beautiful, impressive and inspiring. They have been compiled -With great reverence and Love, and they are capable of appealing to the deepest religious instincts of all classes of people. We have actually malisect all the rever- ence and religious atmosphere which are com- monly associated with the older Anglican and Roman Catholic Churches, and this we have done in freedom and without any suspicion o; superstition. Declining the repetition of formal creeds, in which we cannot all join whole- heartedly, without obedience to some external authority which imposes/beliefs and ideas, we have found a true ideal of' worship, in which there is reverence, beauty, praise and Christian aspiration. Ours is the true Apostolic type of. Church, 'broad enough to include all who seek the highest way of life, and catholic in its temper and atmosphere. And a Church of this character is something ,to be reverenced and treasured, for it is the type to which all Churches, whether of the so-called Free or Established order, are slowly, but surely, tend- ing. But because our Church is free, and ready for the reception of truth from whatever quarter it may come, that does not mean that we have no principles or no beliefs. In our services and in our worship we are continually expressing our beliefs, and these are quite definite and positive. In our hymns and prayers we are con- tinually asserting our absolute moral and spiritual loyalty to our Lord Jesus Christ as the Son of God, and our faith in Christ iig God's supreme gift for the salvation of the world, and we are always declaring our solemn resolution by divine grace to abide in that faith. We have, therefore, a definite Chriftian Church, and though we .do not repeat the orthodox creeds, or bind ourselves to certain articles of religion, we are in the true line of tradition and have real historic continuity with the Apostolic Church

. . . "We 'should like to join in the great work of uniting all Christians in a Free Church, in which all sects and divisions have dis- appeared, and which will acknowledge the ever- present revelation of God in humanity as well as His supreme • revelation -in Christ. Our Church has always stood ,for this broad fee Christian- Catholicism, ever ready to adypnce and welcome the future, yet reverently ccept- ing the heritage of history and tradition and the consolidating power of Christian institutions. In order that we may realise our ideals I ask for your help and encouragement during the coming year,:"

===== 0002601_19160902_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
CENTENARIAN'S DEATH.

Mr. James Chater Succumbs To Bronchitis.

HIS LIFE STORY.

Having lived to within less than a month of 101 years of age, Mr. James Choler, the Warrington centenarian, passed away on the 25th of August at the residence of his daugh- ter; Mrs. Cummins, 65„Buttermarket-street. His birthday was September 26th; 1815, so that he has lived right through the most remark- able century in English history. He had naturally been in failing health for some time Past, but his condition only became serious on July 10th, when he had a seizure. About a fortnight afterwards he regained his faculties and remained in possession of them until Wed- nesday of last Week, when an attack of bron- chitis hastened the end. •

The funeral took place. on Tuesday .at the Warrington Cemetery, when the Rev. J. M. Buckma-ster' read the service. The chief mourners were Mrs. Cummins (daughter), Mrs. Dunning (step-daughter), Mr. and Mrs. James Cummins, Mr. and Mrs. Everell; Mr. and Mrs. McWhirter (grand-children), Mr. Nicholls, of. Weaverham, Mr. and Mrs. Adlerd, Mr., and Mrs. H. Grimoldby, of East Halton, Lincoln- shire, and a number of friends. Flowers were sent by: The Mayor and Mayoress • (Alderman and. Mrs. P. Peacock), Mrs. Cummins; Mr. and Mrs. James Cummins, Mr. and Mrs. W. McWhirter, Mrs. McWhirter and •family, Mr. and Mrs. Williams, -Mr. and Mrs. Sudlow (Fearnhead), Miss White, Mr. and Mrs. Welsby, Mr. and Mrs. Woods, " Clifford and Emily," and Mr. and Mrs. Grimoldby.

===== 0002601_19160902_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS LIFE STORY.

Mr. Chater lived through six reigns. When he reached his hundredth birthday last year he was interviewed for "The Examiner" by Mr. Arthur Mounfield, whose article was sub- sequently read with much interest. The fol- lowing quotations from that article have a renewed attraction at the 'present time Mr. James Chater Was born amid the buzz that marked the downfall of Napoleon, was a

grown youth when Stevenson ran his first locomotive, has seen the world encircled by railways, by the telegraph, telephone, and "wireless," saw the infancy of steam traction, and has lived to gee the maturity of electricity and petrol. A list of the social changes which such a life has covered makes a centenarian seem a survival from another age and realm. Mr. Chater's life has known none of the Vicissitudes which such, a whirling century would suggest. It has been placid and com- paratively uneventful. The trade of a cabinet maker (interesting and conducive to longevity if,anything is) found him occupation and has kept his interest right to the end. Saw Wellington and Scott.

He began his career on September 26th, 1815, in the High-street of Sunderland, in a house next to the "Grey Horse." An 'outstanding memory of his youth was the visit of the Duke of Wellington and Sir Walter-Scott to the town in 1827. Arches' spannfd the streets, ladies in white. stood to Make a welcome, and when the horses Were 'taken from the carriages to allow the Duke to be pulled through the carriages, by admirers, he gave a hand at one of the ropes. He travelled in an open truck upon Steven- son's first railway from Stockton to Darlington, but knew of an earlier railway from Sunderland to Ryhope, on which the conveyances 'were pulled by. ropes. On one occasion he was one of a patient crew who waited for the trucks to begin to move. Patience was needed on all , occasions on ancient railways. In this instance it failed to have its reward. . They waited long without feeling the slightest tug or movement. Then. a discovery was made. The engine had started and wound its ,customary fathoms of rope, but someone had omitted the formality of attaching the hook to the waggon. It is curious to relate that Mr. Chater was regarded in youth as of delicate and unpromis- ing health. But early fears were falsified in his case, and old age found him actively cheer- ful and undisposed to quarrel with Warrington or its climate. Mr. Chater could then write without the aid of glasses, and gave no special trouble to his daughter, into whose care a kindly fortune had placed him in his mature years. •

OTHER LOCAL CENTENARIANS.

Warrington has been productive of many centenarians, as evidenced by the following list Copied from Burial Registers at the Parish Church from 1760 to 1812:—Ralph Boardman, .yeoman, buried December 13th, 1767, aged 104; John Evenson, buried January sth, 1769, aged 100; Mary Rowson, buried December 3rd, 1772, aged 100; Ellen Usherwood, widow, buried January '24th, 1774, aged 100; Betty Blewitt, widow, buried March 22nd, 1785, aged 106; John Stirrup, weaver,' buried August 18th, 1792, aged 100; Margaret Boardman, Widow, buried January 14th, 1803, aged 100; Jane Greg, widow, buried September 13th, 1805, aged 116; Ralph Tootell, smith, buried November 28th, 1806, aged 106. . It will be remembered that Miss Mary Burton, of 244, Manchester-road, passed away in January, 1911, in her 103rd year.

WELCOME AT THE FRONT. Rush Our Soldiers Make For

" The Examiner."

Have you ever been away for a few days or weeks, and wondered how things are going on at home? Few there are who have not ex- perienceCl,the pangs of news-hunger—news from home and the friends still there. How much keener will be the desire of oar soldier boys in the trenches, .the rest camps, .the hospitals, who have been away from home for months or years and are likely to be away for a further 'considerable period.' Private James David Scott, of the South Lancashires, son of Mr. David ,Seott; 61, Kim- berley-street, foreman over the boys at Long- ford Wire Mills, writing to his parents the other day from France, .says:—

Many thanks for " Examiner" just received, after waiting eight days. You can hardly realise how welcome it is out here. When I receive it the boys say " Is that 'The Examiner '?" and when I answer

===== 0002601_19160902_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
" -Yes "I; iiii '' ' there IS a rush for news."

Are ~you, helping to satisfy this craving for news, the intensity of which you can hardly realise? Thousands are doing so. If you are not. begin at once by sending 'The Examiner weekly.

OXFORD LOCAL EXAMINA- TIONS.

Many Successes And Some

Distinctions.

The results of the July Oxford local examina- tions were issued on Thursday and Friday. Boys being examined at 249 centres, an increase of 6 compared with last year, and girls at 297 centres, an increase of 13. The total number of candidates (exclusive of certain overseas centres) examined for ordinary certificates was 18,694, as against 17,834 if 1915—namely, 2,462 preliminary, 6,921 junior, and 9,311 senior. Of these, 1,945 preliminary,. 5,135 junior, and 6,092 senior passed, making a total of 13,172, as corn- parell with 12,845 last year. The total number of candidates and other persons entered for the examination wais 19,799, of whom 287 failed to present themselves for examination, and 836 were entered for special subjects only.

RAILWAY TRAGEDY.

Warrington Inspector Killed At

Birkdale.

A very painful accident occuried yesterday on the Cheshire Lines Railway near Bigkdale Station, resulting in the death of two persons, one of whom was Mr. Wm. Joynson, who re- sided at 22, Brookland-streeti off Padgate-lane, Warrington. It appears that Mr. Joynson, who was an Acting-Inspector, 14W' engaged on the line with a foreman platelayer named James Ball, of'Birkdale, when, unaware of the approach of the 2.30 Southp'Ort to Manchester train, they were knocked down by the engine •and killed.

Mr. Joynson, who has been in the employ of the company for about thirty years, met with an accident some years ago, receiving tn- juries to the head and leg. He leaves a widow, three daughters, and a son, who is serving at the Front with the A.S.C.

RAILWAY FARE ALLOWED.

STAGELAND.

Royal Court Theatre.

• That charming and quaint musical comedY, "Miss Hook of Holland," has taken on with Warrington audiences as captivatingly as when first introduced , here several years ago. It 19 a production particularly popular in the borough, • for the Warrington Musical SocietY included it in itsi repertoire a few years hcit.,k•

Mr. Henry Dallas and Mr. Louis ,Casson have got together a,n excellent company of actors ILO singers. The singing of the chorus is of a first- class order, being exceedingly sweet and 'characterised by lightness of style. Just whoel' is required in fact. As Miss Hook, Miss Edith Delaney achieves considerable distinction, !especially when one remembers that she has only played the part for a month. MisS Delaney's career has mainly been .devoted tO singing ballads on the music halls in London, though several years' ago. she was on tour ih the chorus of Robert Courtneidge's " Dairy- maids." Besides her personal attractiveness 00 the stage, what strikes one particularly is the quality of her singing. Until Christmas, when she entered pantomime, she rested from the stage' for two years, spending her whole tiine in studying the vocal art. • •

The male principals are also of conspicuous merit. I noted with pleasure that Mr. Frank Henry, who took the part of Dr. Isaacson in "Bella Donna" a few months ago, has re- turned to his old love—musical comedy. When he was here before he told me he had to give" up musical comedy because ol the strenuous' ness of this class of stage work. His health, happily, has improved, and he is now revelling in the vigorous personality, of Captain Adrian Paap. Mr. Sam Lockridge as the Bandmaster. has made this part his own, and Mr. Edmuna Russell is a capital Mr. Hook, and Mr. W. Y. Sheen as Slinks, the loafer, introduces much al the drollery characteristic of his well-knowo success as the dame in pantomime.

It :is en a grand scale, and has a "star" cast, including George Hestor, who created such a furore when in "The Arcadians" at Shaftesbury Avenue. Then there are Harry Ray `(another famoug gentleman from the Shaftesbury Theatre), Misig Beatrice Allen, and Miss Lily St. John.

The production, by-the-bYe, is presented by Mr. Jackson, by arrangement with Mr. Fred Karno, whose name in tilt' revue world is one to conjure with. Tenders. THE RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL OF R UNCORN. CONTRACTS FOR PROVISIONS AND SUPPLIES. . The above-named Council invite TENDERS for the supply Of BUTCHER'S MEAT, BREAD; GROCERIES, MILK, and FUEL, to their Isolation Hospital at Dutton, daring the half- year ending 31st March. 1917, to be delivered in snail auantities and at such times as shall be required by the Matron of the Hospital. Tender forms may be obtained from, and all tenders must be sent in to me at .71, High-street, Runcorn, not later than Monday, the 4th day of September, 1916, endorsed "Tender for W. E. HOUGH, Acting Clerk to the Council. Council Offices, Runcorn, 24th August, 1916. RUNCORN UNION. CONTRACTS FOR, PROVISIONS AND SUPPLIES FOR THE HALF-YEAR ENDING MARCH 31sT, 1917. The Guardians of the Poor of the Runcorn Union are desirous of receiving TENDRILS from parties willing to Contract for the supply of— Good Beef, to consist of vein pieces intact and Flat Ribs with bone, one round weekly not tc exceed 251 b., and 451 b. per week of the best Butcher's Meat, including Veal, Lamb, and Pork (in season) for the Officers, , Sides of Mutton (all meat to be English killed), Bread, Spirits, Groceries, , • Tobacco I; Snuff, Drapery, Earthenware, Clothing, • Oils & Colours, Boots,_ Coal, Slack, Coke, Boot Repaixinga Deal Coffins, Drysaltery, Horse Hire Brushes & Mats, (for funerals Si carting) Wines, • Tenders are also invited from Tradesmen for the services per hour of Journeymen and Apprentices of the following trades :—Pliimber, Joiner, Whitesmith, and Bricklayer. Parties tendering for the supply of the above- mentioned Goods will be required to deliver the same at the Workhouse and Children's Home, both situate in Dutton, free of cost to the Guardians, how and when required. Tenders to be accompanied with samples (ex- cept Drapery Goods and Clothing) as far as practicable. The Guardians do not bind them- selves to accept the lowest or any tender. Persons desirous of tendering must apply by letter to my office, in Runcorn, for a printed Form of Tender (and none other can be enter- tained), where a Schedule of the various articles required and further particulars may be obtained. Sealed Tenders, addressed to the Guardians, to be forwarded to the Clerk's Office, Runcorn, on or before Monday, the 4th day of Sept., 1916, endorsed "Tender for The Tenders will be opened and considered at a meeting of the Guardians to be held in the Board Room, Dutton; on the sth day of Sept., 1916, at 1 p.m. Parties whose tenders are accepted must enter into a written 'contract, and be prepared to enter into a bond, with two sureties, if required by the Guardians. By order of the Board, W. E. HOUGH, Acting Clerk to the Guardians. Runcorn, 24th August, 1916.

about to give. To Germany the intervention, both from the military and the political side, is of the gravest import. Germans, who have wooed and threatened in turn, now turn to abuse. The Roumanians are. now "perfi- dious." Well, so are Englishmen accotding to the stunted German ideas—and birds of a feather naturally get together, just as the un- speakable Hun joined the unspeakable Turk. The only thing wanting now is that Greece should prove perfidigus, too, and "join up."

PRIVATE HAROLD PARTRIDGE. Wounded.

sub-captain of the first team, Harold was an exceedingly useful batsman to the club, and the many admirers of his prowess will hope for him a speedy recovery. Particulars as to the nature of his wounds have not yet been received. Enlisting on the 28th March, he has been in France six weeks, leaving the office of 'Mr. T. F. W. Dixon, iron and steel merchant, of Manchester and Lymm,s`to do so. He was edu- cated at Arpley-street School and the Secondary School. He is a splendid swimmer, and when at People's College won the schoolboys' cham- pionship. He is niarried, and his wife and child reside in Folly-lane.' eldest brother, Sergeant Stanley Part- ridge, is still in England, where he has been since sustaining severe wounds in France in February. Yet another brother, Corporal Fred, is in, the Army, too. PRIVATE GEORGE MORRIS. "6iive Germhuns More Than They Gave Him." PRIVATE F. EVANS. Wounded.

the greater majority of the houses occupied by the working class are not blessed with a private bath. They have to bathe and wash in their bedrooms, or wait till dusk, and go to some water near by, in most cases the lake, 'and risk falling into the hands of the local police. Yet when all these facts are put before you still you go on in the usual way.‘ Be very • careful- atiout!the beauty of Lyram ! Never mind the convenience of the working class, and be very careful about those rates. Some day, perhaps, you will understand. PRIVATE WILLIAM READ. Nob‘‘ fields Soldier Wounded In The Right Arm.
